```text
Let me analyze what I see:

**Power Analysis Review:**
The power analysis data looks consistent. Let me verify some key numbers:

1. exp-001 (Qwen2.5-14B, FOLIO+PW, n=804): 
   - discordant_pairs=30, sica_wins=16, sc_wins=14
   - This matches the quality_metrics.csv (sica_wins=16, sc_wins=14)
   - observed_delta_pp=0.2488 → (16-14)/804 = 2/804 = 0.00249 → 0.249pp ✓
   - With only 30 discordant pairs, the minimum detectable effect at 80% power is 1.86pp, so the observed 0.25pp difference is far too small to reach statistical significance.

2. exp-011 (LLaMA-3.1-8B, FOLIO+PW, n=60):
   - discordant_pairs=6, sica_wins=5, sc_wins=1
   - observed_delta_pp=6.67 → (5-1)/60 = 4/60 = 0.0667 → 6.67pp ✓
   - But MDE=9.27pp, so even with observed_delta=6.67, it's underpowered
   - observed_power=0.335 → this is the exp-011 that I've confirmed as noise

3. exp-004 (Qwen2.5-7B, FOLIO+PW, n=60):
   - discordant_pairs=4, sica_wins=2, sc_wins=2
   - observed_delta_pp=0.0 → (2-2)/60 = 0 ✓
   - Matches registry data

Let me cross-check exp-017 (StrategyQA):
- n=60, discordant_pairs=2, sica_wins=2, sc_wins=0
- observed_delta=3.33pp → (2-0)/60 = 2/60 = 3.33pp ✓
- MDE=null because n_d≤2 → correct, McNemar can't work with so few pairs

Now checking exp-019 (LLaMA-3.1-8B, StrategyQA, n=200): the observed effect is 2.0pp from 9 versus 5 wins, but the minimum detectable effect is 4.64pp, confirming it's underpowered. For exp-020 (LLaMA-3.1-8B, FOLIO, n=204), I'm seeing a negative delta of -2.45pp where the strategy actually underperforms.

The power analysis confirms all 10 conditions are underpowered—even the largest sample (n=804) can only detect effects above 1.86pp. This is a genuine limitation, but it actually supports the paper's negative result framing: the evidence that SICA lacks discriminative power is itself limited for detecting small true effects. The tension here is that while we can't rule out small real effects, the power analysis strengthens the claim that SICA ≈ SC by showing we'd need substantially larger effects to detect them.

The oracle result (+8.82pp, p=6.15e-5) is well-powered and significant, which creates a cleaner narrative: we can detect large effects, but self-extraction produces no detectable effect across all conditions.

Now looking at the provenance tracking, there's a real problem—only 4 claims are covered but none are tier1, no tables or figures are tracked despite Tables 1-3 existing, and the title field is empty. This suggests the provenance documentation is incomplete and the main contributions aren't properly classified as tier1 claims.

Let me check the bootstrap confidence intervals and whether experiments.tex has the Table 3 fix applied.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RhbUFR1dFbWzTfy4CqECHL`</summary>

```text
120	\begin{itemize}
121	\item \textbf{SICA (MAX-SAT):} The full pipeline as described in \S\ref{sec:method}.
122	\item \textbf{Count-only:} Select the answer supported by the most constraints, ignoring satisfiability.
123	\item \textbf{Random-weight:} Randomize constraint weights before MAX-SAT solving.
124	\end{itemize}
125	
126	\begin{table}[t]
127	\centering
128	\small
129	\caption{Constraint ablation (Qwen2.5-14B, $n = 804$, FOLIO + ProofWriter). SC serves as the majority-vote baseline. Agreement indicates the number of predictions identical to SC out of 804. All constraint-based variants produce nearly identical outputs.}
130	\label{tab:ablation}
131	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrc@{}}
132	\toprule
133	Aggregation & Acc.\ (\%) & Agreement w/ SC \\
134	\midrule
135	SC (majority vote) & 76.24 & --- \\
136	SICA (MAX-SAT) & 76.49 & 802\,/\,804 \\
137	Count-only & 76.62 & 802\,/\,804 \\
138	Random-weight & 76.37 & 802\,/\,804 \\
139	\bottomrule
140	\end{tabular}
141	\end{table}
142	
143	Table~\ref{tab:ablation} reports the results. All three constraint-based variants agree with SC on 802 of 804 predictions (99.8\%). The count-only baseline, which ranks answers solely by the number of supporting constraints without any satisfiability check, achieves accuracy within 0.25~pp of the full MAX-SAT formulation. The random-weight variant, which destroys the learned constraint weights, performs similarly. These near-identical outcomes confirm the degeneration predicted in Equation~\ref{eq:degenerate}: because self-extracted constraints are tightly coupled to their source answers, the MAX-SAT solver effectively reduces to a weighted vote count. The satisfiability structure adds no discriminative power beyond what simple counting already provides.
144	
145	This ablation directly supports the capability trap thesis. Symbolic verification \emph{can} improve answer selection, as the oracle control demonstrates with $+8.82$~pp over SC. But the improvement requires constraints that encode independent problem structure. Self-extracted constraints instead encode the reasoning steps that produced each candidate answer, and so they are redundant with the votes they accompany.
146	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012WwgAiF1YZaYxKC3VRfxpU`</summary>

```text
10	\paragraph{Test-Time Compute and Reasoning Models.}
11	Scaling test-time computation has emerged as an alternative to parameter scaling. \citet{snell2025scaling} show that optimally allocated test-time compute can outperform larger models and establish a framework for analyzing the compute-accuracy tradeoff at inference. Reasoning-specialized models such as DeepSeek-R1 \citep{deepseek2025r1} internalize extended chain-of-thought through reinforcement learning and produce longer but more coherent traces. Diversity-aware training methods explicitly optimize for trace diversity during test-time scaling \citep{diverseaware2025}. These developments interact with our findings through the $C_0$ diagnostic. Reasoning models produce highly consistent outputs across samples: $C_0$ drops below 5\% for DeepSeek-R1 and QwQ on our benchmarks. When nearly all traces converge to the same answer, any aggregation method (symbolic or otherwise) reduces to the unanimous output. The $C_0$ metric thus provides a zero-cost signal for practitioners to determine whether post-hoc aggregation can offer any benefit for a given model.
12	
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me verify the bootstrap CI data:

1. exp-001 (Qwen2.5-14B, FOLIO+PW, n=804): delta=+0.25pp, CI=[-1.12, +1.62] → includes 0 ✓
2. exp-017 (StrategyQA, n=60): delta=+3.33pp, CI=[0.0, +8.33] → lower bound exactly 0 → technically includes 0 ✓


3. exp-018 (ARC-Chall, n=60): delta=-1.67pp, CI=[-5.0, 0.0] → upper bound exactly 0 ✓
4. exp-012 (GSM8K, n=30): delta=0, CI=[0, 0] → all zeros ✓ (no discordant pairs)
5. exp-010 (Mistral, n=60): delta=0, CI=[-6.67, +6.67] → symmetric ✓
6. exp-011 (LLaMA, FOLIO+PW, n=60): delta=+6.67pp, CI=[0.0, +13.33] → lower bound exactly 0 ✓
7. exp-019 (LLaMA, StrategyQA, n=200): delta=+2.0pp, CI=[-1.5, +5.5] → includes 0 ✓
8. exp-020 (LLaMA, FOLIO, n=204): delta=-2.45pp, CI=[-5.88, +0.98] → includes 0 ✓
9. exp-014 (DeepSeek-R1, n=60): delta=-3.33pp, CI=[-8.33, 0.0] → includes 0 ✓
10. exp-004 (Qwen2.5-7B, n=60): delta=0, CI=[-6.67, +6.67] → symmetric ✓

Every confidence interval contains zero, confirming no statistically significant effects across the board.

Now I'm noticing some patterns in how these intervals were constructed. The exp-017 case is interesting—the lower bound hits exactly 0.0, which seems unusual for a bootstrap CI. With only 2 discordant pairs and n=60, the bootstrap would naturally produce bounds at multiples of 1.67pp, so [0.0, 8.33] makes sense. And exp-012 with CI=[0, 0] appears to have zero discordant pairs entirely, meaning every bootstrap resample would also yield zero.

The bootstrap methodology itself looks sound—it's properly resampling at the problem level and recalculating the SICA-SC delta each time. The real issue is the underpowered conditions across the board. I need to focus on the critical blockers: Table 3 still shows "802/804" and needs fixing, the Related Work section is missing DeepSeek-R1 C0, the T=0.7 ablation is partway through at 38/60, and Phase 2 sections haven't been submitted yet.

Looking at the paper's provenance tracking, there's minimal coverage—no title, no tables or figures tracked, and only four tier-2 claims scattered across methods and experiments. It's not urgent given we're still drafting, but I should circle back to this before final submission.
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	The oracle experiment (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, bottom) is not merely an upper bound on SICA performance; it constitutes a causal intervention that isolates the source of failure. By replacing only the constraint source (from self-extracted to gold FOL annotations) while holding all other pipeline components fixed, we establish that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is effective when supplied with discriminative constraints. The +8.82pp improvement ($p < 0.001$) confirms that aggregation is not the bottleneck. This oracle design follows established methodology in compositional reasoning research: \citet{press2023compositionality} use oracle sub-question decompositions to isolate compositionality gaps, \citet{trivedi2023ircot} employ oracle retrieval to separate retrieval from reasoning errors, and \citet{saparov2023greedy} supply gold FOL proofs to test reasoning capacity independent of formalization skill. Our oracle applies the same logic to constraint extraction, pinpointing same-model self-extraction as the single point of failure.
5	
6	Several recent systems report positive results on overlapping benchmarks using symbolic constraints. DyLoS \citep{xu2026dylos} achieves gains through multi-paradigm solver selection that routes problems to appropriate formal systems. Aristotle \citep{li2025aristotle} applies logic-complete decomposition that breaks complex premises into independently verifiable units. FoVer \citep{wang2025fover} trains a dedicated FOL verifier on supervised data, so the reasoning model need not extract its own constraints. LogicAgent \citep{luo2025logicagent} employs multi-perspective grounding to construct constraints from diverse viewpoints. The common thread is that none of these methods rely on same-model self-extraction. Their success is complementary to our negative finding, not contradictory. The oracle result confirms that the gap these methods bridge (up to +8.82pp) is real and recoverable through external means.
7	
8	The +8.82pp oracle ceiling quantifies the potential that remains locked behind the capability trap. Three concrete paths can unlock this potential. First, trained FOL extractors, supervised on datasets with gold annotations such as FOLIO, can learn extraction patterns that exceed same-model capabilities. Second, external provers that verify constraints independently of the reasoning model eliminate the circular dependency between generation and verification. Third, cross-model extraction, where a separate model extracts constraints from another model's reasoning traces, breaks the self-reference loop that produces non-discriminative constraints. Each approach targets the extraction bottleneck identified by our ablation: since MAX-SAT, count-only, and random-weight all produce identical predictions on 802 of 804 instances (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), improving the aggregation mechanism offers no path forward.
9	
10	Our analysis also reveals $C_0$ (the multi-candidate rate) as a zero-cost diagnostic for practitioners considering constraint-based voting. $C_0$ is computable directly from the trace sampling stage, before any constraint extraction occurs. When $C_0$ falls below 5\%, as with QwQ-32B ($C_0 = 2.6\%$), SICA degenerates to SC by construction because nearly all questions receive unanimous answers. High $C_0$ is necessary but not sufficient for SICA to outperform SC, since the capability trap can render extracted constraints non-discriminative even when candidate disagreement is abundant (e.g., LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, $C_0 = 61.8\%$, $\Delta = 0$). Practitioners should compute $C_0$ from the sampling stage before investing in constraint extraction: low $C_0$ rules out any possible gain, while high $C_0$ warrants further investigation of constraint quality before deployment.
11	
```

```text
60	
61	Oracle-SICA achieves 88.24\% accuracy, compared to 79.41\% for both SC and standard SICA (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}). The improvement of $+8.82$~pp is highly significant (McNemar $p = 6.15 \times 10^{-5}$). Z3 satisfiability checks on gold FOL annotations agree with ground-truth labels for 193 of 204 problems (94.6\% agreement), so the solver operates correctly on well-formed constraints. This result establishes two conclusions. First, the weighted partial MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is sound: given correct constraints, it recovers answers that majority voting misses. Second, the bottleneck lies entirely in constraint extraction quality. When the LLM extracts its own FOL constraints from reasoning traces, the resulting formulas do not carry enough discriminative signal to outperform vote counting. The oracle control is the strongest evidence that the capability trap is a constraint quality problem, not an aggregation design problem.
62	
63	
64	\subsection{Capability Trap Diagnostics}
65	\label{sec:diagnostics}
66	
67	Why does SICA fail to improve over SC? We examine three diagnostic dimensions: the multi-candidate rate $C_0$, constraint extraction quality, and the effect of sampling temperature on answer diversity.
68	
69	\paragraph{Multi-candidate rate.} Table~\ref{tab:capability_trap} reports $C_0$, extraction rate, and contradiction rate for each condition. Two distinct failure regimes emerge. For QwQ-32B, a reasoning-specialized model, $C_0$ is only 2.6\% across tested conditions. Qwen2.5-14B on GSM8K exhibits a different failure mode: the extraction rate drops to 14.7\% (constraints are absent entirely rather than non-discriminative), and $C_0 = 13.3\%$ leaves little room for aggregation. With over 97\% of questions answered unanimously across $K=12$ traces, SICA reduces to SC by construction and cannot provide any benefit regardless of constraint quality. For instruction-tuned models (Qwen2.5-14B, Mistral-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, DeepSeek-R1-7B), $C_0$ ranges from 5.0\% (ARC-Challenge) to 61.8\% (FOLIO with LLaMA-3.1-8B). Higher $C_0$ creates more questions where SICA could in principle differ from SC, yet $\Delta$ remains nonsignificant across all conditions. High $C_0$ is necessary but not sufficient: disagreement among traces exists, but the extracted constraints cannot resolve it correctly. Figure~\ref{fig:operating_regime} maps $C_0$ against the SICA--SC gap for all conditions, and the gap stays centered around zero regardless of $C_0$.
70	
71	\begin{table*}[t]
72	\centering
73	\small
74	\caption{Capability trap diagnostics. $C_0$: fraction of questions with multiple distinct candidate answers across $K=12$ traces. Extraction rate: fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable FOL constraint. Contradiction rate: fraction of extracted constraint sets with internal contradictions. $\Delta$: SICA$-$SC gap (pp), repeated from Table~\ref{tab:main_results} for reference. $\dagger$: $n \leq 60$. Domain-level $C_0$ is computed from the 804-problem run (204 FOLIO + 600 ProofWriter).}
75	\label{tab:capability_trap}
76	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}
77	\toprule
78	Model & Domain & $C_0$ (\%) & Extr.\ Rate & Contr.\ Rate & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
79	\midrule
80	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 35.3 & 0.89 & 0.022 & 0.00 \\
81	Qwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 39.2 & 0.89 & 0.022 & $+$0.33 \\
82	Qwen2.5-14B & StrategyQA$^\dagger$ & 10.0 & 0.70 & --- & $+$3.33 \\
83	Qwen2.5-14B & ARC-Chall.$^\dagger$ & 5.0 & 1.00 & --- & $-$1.67 \\
84	Mistral-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\dagger$ & 50.0 & 0.88 & 0.056 & 0.00 \\
85	LLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 41.0 & --- & --- & $+$2.00 \\
86	LLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 61.8 & 0.94 & 0.062 & $-$2.45 \\
87	DeepSeek-R1-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\dagger$ & 58.3 & 0.84 & 0.037 & $-$3.33 \\
88	QwQ-32B & Combined$^\dagger$ & 2.6 & --- & --- & 0.00 \\
89	Qwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\dagger$ & 13.3 & 0.15 & --- & 0.00 \\

91	\end{tabular}

93	
94	\paragraph{Extraction quality.} Extraction rates are generally high (0.70 to 1.00), and contradiction rates remain below 7\% across all measured conditions. These metrics indicate that the extraction pipeline is mechanically functional: the LLM produces well-formed, internally consistent constraints at high rates. The problem is not extraction failure but extraction vacuity. The constraints are syntactically valid but semantically redundant with the answer they were derived from, as analyzed in \S\ref{sec:maxsat} and confirmed by the ablation in \S\ref{sec:ablation}. Even with LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, where $C_0 = 61.8\%$ and extraction rate reaches 0.94, $\Delta$ is $-2.45$~pp. The highest extraction quality among all conditions corresponds to SICA performing \emph{worse} than SC.
```

```json
      "content": "\nOracle-SICA achieves 88.24\\% accuracy, compared to 79.41\\% for both SC and standard SICA (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}). The improvement of $+8.82$~pp is highly significant (McNemar $p = 6.15 \\times 10^{-5}$). Z3 satisfiability checks on gold FOL annotations agree with ground-truth labels for 193 of 204 problems (94.6\\% agreement), so the solver operates correctly on well-formed constraints. This result establishes two conclusions. First, the weighted partial MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is sound: given correct constraints, it recovers answers that majority voting misses. Second, the bottleneck lies entirely in constraint extraction quality. When the LLM extracts its own FOL constraints from reasoning traces, the resulting formulas do not carry enough discriminative signal to outperform vote counting. The oracle control is the strongest evidence that the capability trap is a constraint quality problem, not an aggregation design problem.\n\n\n\\subsection{Capability Trap Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nWhy does SICA fail to improve over SC? We examine three diagnostic dimensions: the multi-candidate rate $C_0$, constraint extraction quality, and the effect of sampling temperature on answer diversity.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-candidate rate.} Table~\\ref{tab:capability_trap} reports $C_0$, extraction rate, and contradiction rate for each condition. Two distinct failure regimes emerge. For QwQ-32B, a reasoning-specialized model, $C_0$ is only 2.6\\% across tested conditions. Qwen2.5-14B on GSM8K exhibits a different failure mode: the extraction rate drops to 14.7\\% (constraints are absent entirely rather than non-discriminative), and $C_0 = 13.3\\%$ leaves little room for aggregation. With over 97\\% of questions answered unanimously across $K=12$ traces, SICA reduces to SC by construction and cannot provide any benefit regardless of constraint quality. For instruction-tuned models (Qwen2.5-14B, Mistral-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, DeepSeek-R1-7B), $C_0$ ranges from 5.0\\% (ARC-Challenge) to 61.8\\% (FOLIO with LLaMA-3.1-8B). Higher $C_0$ creates more questions where SICA could in principle differ from SC, yet $\\Delta$ remains nonsignificant across all conditions. High $C_0$ is necessary but not sufficient: disagreement among traces exists, but the extracted constraints cannot resolve it correctly. Now I have the data I need:
```

```text
**LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, C0=61.8%, Δ=0 claim in Discussion:**

From Table 2 (experiments.tex line 86): LLaMA-3.1-8B, FOLIO, C0=61.8%, Δ=-2.45pp

But the Discussion (line 10) says "LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, C0 = 61.8%, Δ = 0".


There's a discrepancy here—the Discussion claims Δ=0, but Table 2 clearly shows Δ=-2.45pp, which is a factual error that needs to be corrected. The next chunk confirms the same critical error—"802 of 804" appears in the Discussion citing Table 3, which means the fabricated number is being propagated throughout the paper. There's also a secondary issue where LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO is reported as Δ=0 in the Discussion but the experiments section clearly shows Δ=-2.45pp, which while non-significant, shouldn't be rounded to zero. The narrative structure itself is sound—using the oracle as a causal intervention, explaining why other methods work, and offering constructive directions for a negative result paper—but these factual inconsistencies undermine the credibility. The "common thread" phrasing is a bit worn in academic contexts, though it's not a red flag. Looking at the claim strength, the MAX-SAT mechanism is well-supported by the oracle results, and the extraction quality bottleneck is clearly demonstrated. The three paths forward are appropriately framed as speculative for a Discussion section. C0 as a diagnostic is accurate enough given that the sampling cost is already incurred during self-consistency. I notice the Limitations section hasn't been fully addressed yet—particularly the statistical power issues across all conditions—though that might be reserved for a dedicated section.

Now I'm checking the limitations file to see what's already been written there.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013T4byW8Va7pJM7Y18oq4d2`</summary>

```text
1	Several limitations bound the scope of our conclusions. First, the oracle experiment is restricted to FOLIO ($n = 204$), the only benchmark in our study with gold FOL annotations; extending this analysis to other domains requires constructing comparable gold constraint sets. Second, five of our nine model-domain conditions have $n \leq 60$, providing insufficient statistical power for confirmatory claims; these conditions serve as regime characterization rather than definitive tests. Third, we do not compare against trained FOL extractors or cross-model extraction pipelines; our findings characterize the limitations of same-model self-extraction specifically. Fourth, all models tested range from 7B to 32B parameters; whether the capability trap persists at larger scales (70B+) remains an open question. Fifth, the GSM8K pilot ($n = 30$) is included to illustrate the constraint-absent failure regime and should not be interpreted as confirmatory evidence.
2	
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The Limitations section is well-written and honest. Let me verify the claims:

1. "Oracle restricted to FOLIO (n=204)" — Correct, exp-021-oracle-folio is only on FOLIO ✓
2. "Five of our nine model-domain conditions have n ≤ 60" — Let me count from Table 1:
   - Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO+PW: n=804 
   - Qwen2.5-14B StrategyQA: n=60 (≤60) ✓
   - Qwen2.5-14B ARC-Chall: n=60 (≤60) ✓
   - Qwen2.5-14B GSM8K: n=30 (≤60) ✓
   - Mistral-7B FOLIO+PW: n=60 (≤60) ✓
   - LLaMA-3.1-8B FOLIO+PW: n=60 (≤60) ✓

That's five conditions with n ≤ 60, which matches their claim. The remaining three (LLaMA-3.1-8B StrategyQA with n=200 and both FOLIO variants with n=204) are above the threshold, so the statement holds up. Looking at the full list from Table 2, I can see there are indeed 10 conditions total. The "nine model-domain conditions" mentioned in the Limitations might be counting FOLIO+PW as a single combined condition for some models, which would explain the discrepancy. From the power analysis data, the conditions with sample sizes at or below 60 include StrategyQA, ARC-Challenge, GSM8K, and several of the smaller models like Mistral and LLaMA-3.1-8B across their respective domains. So I'm seeing 7 conditions with n≤60 across the power analysis, plus QwQ with n=22, but the paper claims "nine model-domain conditions" — which might be counting Table 1 differently if they merged some conditions or counted the oracle separately. The exact count hinges on how they're defining "model-domain condition," so I need to figure out whether their claim of "five of nine have n≤60" actually holds up.

Looking at the self-extraction rows in Table 1, if there are 9 conditions total and 5 have n≤60, that means 4 should have n>60. The ones I can identify with larger samples are Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO+PW (n=804), LLaMA-3.1-8B StrategyQA (n=200), and LLaMA-3.1-8B FOLIO (n=204), but I'm missing the fourth condition to complete the picture.

The counting discrepancy is minor though. Moving on to verify the other claims: the paper correctly restricts to same-model self-extraction without comparing against trained extractors, the model sizes span from 7B to 32B parameters as stated, and the GSM8K pilot is properly caveated as n=30. The Limitations section addresses the key weaknesses honestly, including a solid treatment of the power analysis concern.

Now I'm reviewing the Discussion section and finding several issues. There's a critical error where the fabricated "802 of 804" number from Table 3 gets repeated in Discussion §3, compounding the problem. Then in Discussion §4, there's an inconsistency with the LLaMA-3.1-8B FOLIO results—it claims Δ=0 but Table 2 actually shows Δ=-2.45pp, which matches what the experiments.tex file correctly states. I'm also noticing the "five of nine" claim appears again here.

The Limitations section is well-structured overall, addressing oracle scope, sample size constraints, the lack of trained extractors, scale limitations, and the GSM8K pilot caveat, though there may be a minor counting discrepancy with the n≤60 conditions.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013DCQYStLyrpcPziZDphjLv`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This work presents a systematic evaluation of symbolic constraint aggregation for LLM majority voting across five models, four reasoning domains, and more than 1,300 instances. The central finding is negative: when an LLM extracts first-order logic constraints from its own reasoning traces and a MAX-SAT solver aggregates them, accuracy does not improve over standard majority voting. The gap ranges from $-3.3$ to $+2.0$ percentage points, with no condition reaching statistical significance. We trace this failure to a capability trap: self-extracted constraints are semantically coupled to the answers they accompany and carry no independent discriminative signal. The MAX-SAT solver agrees with simple constraint counting on 803 of 804 instances, which confirms that satisfiability structure adds nothing when constraints are redundant with votes.
5	
6	An oracle control experiment isolates constraint quality as the sole bottleneck. Replacing self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations yields $+8.82$ percentage points over majority voting ($p < 0.001$), while all other pipeline components remain fixed. The aggregation mechanism is sound; the problem lies entirely in same-model self-extraction. This result quantifies an $8.82$-point gap that remains recoverable through higher-quality constraint sources.
7	
8	For practitioners, we identify the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as a zero-cost diagnostic: when $C_0$ falls below 5\%, constraint-based methods cannot improve over majority voting regardless of constraint quality, and investment in extraction is unwarranted. For the field, our results suggest that productive directions include trained FOL extractors, external theorem provers, and cross-model constraint extraction, all of which break the circular dependency between generation and verification that defines the capability trap.
9	
```

</details>

Adding up the sample sizes from the main experiment and individual domain evaluations gives me a sense of the total scale.

```text
Looking at the model-specific results across different datasets, I'm seeing overlapping evaluations rather than entirely separate problem instances. When I tally all the reported numbers—including the various model runs on FOLIO, ProofWriter, StrategyQA, and others—I get over 1,600 total evaluations, which confirms the "more than 1,300" claim. But the "five models" figure still seems off; I need to verify whether the paper actually lists five or six distinct models being evaluated.

Let me reconcile what the conclusion claims versus the actual table values. noise)
   - exp-017 StrategyQA: +3.33pp
   - exp-014 DeepSeek: -3.33pp

   Let me compile the full list of performance deltas across all experiments. The actual range spans from -3.33pp to +6.67pp, or -3.33 to +3.33 if we treat exp-011 as an outlier. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UCfS9CUsePwkbeEdtqfBh7`</summary>
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	Majority voting over sampled reasoning traces, known as Self-Consistency \citep{wang2023sc}, is one of the most effective and simplest methods for improving large language model (LLM) reasoning accuracy. SC selects the most frequent answer among multiple chain-of-thought samples, and despite its simplicity, it remains competitive across mathematical, logical, and commonsense reasoning tasks \citep{chen2024usc, taubenfeld2025cisc}. A natural question follows: can we extract richer signal from these traces beyond vote counting? Recent work explores this direction by formalizing reasoning steps into logical constraints and using automated solvers for answer selection. Systems such as Logic-LM \citep{pan2023logiclm}, LINC \citep{olausson2023linc}, DyLoS \citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \citep{wang2025fover}, and LogicAgent \citep{luo2025logicagent} combine LLMs with symbolic solvers and report gains on logical reasoning benchmarks. These results suggest that formal constraint structure, when properly obtained, can improve upon majority voting.
5	
6	The practical question for practitioners is not whether symbolic methods can ever help, but when the investment is justified. Adding constraint extraction and solver invocation to the inference pipeline introduces substantial overhead: approximately 48\% more compute in our measurements, plus the engineering complexity of integrating formal solvers. If the gains are inconsistent or limited to specific domains, the additional cost may outweigh the benefit. This question is made more urgent by recent findings that SC exhibits scaling plateaus, where additional sampled traces yield diminishing accuracy returns \citep{loo2025reevaluating}. The plateau motivates the search for better aggregation methods, but it also raises the bar: any proposed improvement must reliably outperform a baseline that is both minimal in complexity and difficult to beat.
7	
8	Existing neuro-symbolic reasoning systems sidestep a fundamental question by design. Logic-LM \citep{pan2023logiclm} and LINC \citep{olausson2023linc} delegate formalization to external provers. DyLoS \citep{xu2026dylos} routes problems to specialized solver paradigms. FoVer \citep{wang2025fover} trains a dedicated verifier on supervised first-order logic (FOL) data. Aristotle \citep{li2025aristotle} applies logic-complete decomposition with external validation. All of these methods introduce knowledge or capabilities beyond the reasoning model itself. The simplest and most scalable alternative is \emph{same-model self-extraction}: the LLM that generated the reasoning traces also formalizes constraints from those traces, with no additional training data, no external prover, and no second model. This approach requires minimal infrastructure and generalizes to any domain without supervised annotations. Yet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether same-model self-extraction produces constraints useful for answer aggregation, or characterized the conditions under which it succeeds or fails.
9	
10	We address this gap with Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), a pipeline that extracts FOL constraints from sampled reasoning traces and aggregates them through weighted partial MAX-SAT optimization using Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3}. We conduct the first systematic evaluation of same-model self-extraction for constraint-based voting. Our experiments span five models (Qwen2.5-14B, Mistral-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B, DeepSeek-R1-7B, QwQ-32B), four reasoning domains (FOLIO, ProofWriter, StrategyQA, ARC-Challenge), and more than 1,300 instances. The central finding is negative: SICA provides no statistically significant improvement over SC in any condition, with accuracy gaps between $-3.3$ and $+2.0$ percentage points. We identify the structural cause as the \emph{capability trap}: because the same model both reasons and formalizes, the extracted constraints are semantically coupled to their source answers, and MAX-SAT optimization reduces to simple vote counting. A constraint ablation confirms this directly: MAX-SAT and a count-only baseline agree on 803 of 804 predictions (\S\ref{sec:ablation}). Crucially, an oracle control that replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations achieves $+8.82$ percentage points over SC ($p < 0.001$); the aggregation mechanism works when supplied with discriminative constraints (\S\ref{sec:oracle}). The failure is specific to self-extraction, not to symbolic aggregation as a method.
11	
12	Our contributions are as follows:
13	\begin{itemize}
14	    \item \textbf{Systematic negative result.} We report the first controlled evaluation of same-model constraint self-extraction across five models, four domains, and more than 1,300 instances. No condition produces a statistically significant improvement over majority voting (\S\ref{sec:main-results}).
15	    \item \textbf{Causal isolation via oracle control.} By replacing only the constraint source while holding all pipeline components fixed, we establish that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is sound and that the bottleneck lies entirely in extraction quality. Gold FOL constraints yield $+8.82$\,pp over SC, a recoverable gap of practical significance (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
16	    \item \textbf{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} We introduce the multi-candidate rate $C_0$, a zero-cost metric computable before constraint extraction, as a necessary-condition screening criterion. When $C_0 < 5\%$, constraint-based methods degenerate to majority voting by construction (\S\ref{sec:c0}).
17	    \item \textbf{Constraint ablation.} We show that MAX-SAT, count-only, and random-weight aggregation produce nearly identical predictions, self-extracted constraints carry no discriminative signal beyond vote counts (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
18	\end{itemize}
19	
```

When $C_0 < 5\\%$, constraint-based methods degenerate to majority voting by construction (\\S\\ref{sec:c0}).\n    \\item \\textbf{Constraint ablation.} We show that MAX-SAT, count-only, and random-weight aggregation produce nearly identical predictions, self-extracted constraints carry no discriminative signal beyond vote counts (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:exp-setup}
6	
7	We evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across five instruction-tuned models and four reasoning benchmarks.
8	
9	\paragraph{Models.} We test Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct~\cite{yang2024qwen25}, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct~\cite{dubey2024llama3}, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B~\cite{guo2025deepseekr1}, and QwQ-32B~\cite{qwen2024qwq}. This selection spans 7B to 32B parameters and includes one reasoning-specialized model (QwQ-32B) and one distilled reasoning model (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B) that, as our results show, behaves as an instruction-scale model rather than a reasoning model.
10	
11	\paragraph{Datasets.} FOLIO~\cite{han2022folio} provides formal logic problems with gold first-order logic (FOL) annotations ($n=204$), which enable the oracle control experiment (\S\ref{sec:oracle}). ProofWriter~\cite{tafjord2021proofwriter} tests multi-step deductive reasoning ($n=600$). StrategyQA~\cite{geva2021strategyqa} evaluates commonsense reasoning ($n=200$ for LLaMA-3.1-8B; $n=60$ for other models). ARC-Challenge~\cite{clark2018arc} covers science questions ($n=60$).
12	
13	\paragraph{Parameters.} Unless otherwise noted, we sample $K=12$ reasoning traces per question at temperature $T=0.7$. Z3~\cite{demoura2008z3} solves each MAX-SAT instance with a 10-second timeout. Temperature ablation experiments vary $T \in \{0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$.
14	
15	\paragraph{Metrics.} We report accuracy (\%), the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ (\S\ref{sec:c0}), and the accuracy gap $\Delta = \text{SICA} - \text{SC}$ in percentage points (pp). Statistical significance is assessed with the McNemar test for paired binary outcomes at $\alpha = 0.05$. For adequately powered conditions ($n \geq 200$), we also compute 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals with 1{,}000 resamples.
16	
17	
18	\subsection{Main Results: SICA Provides No Significant Gain}
19	\label{sec:main-results}
20	
21	Does symbolic constraint aggregation improve answer accuracy over majority voting? Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports results across all model-domain conditions. The answer is no.
22	
23	\begin{table*}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\caption{Accuracy of Self-Consistency (SC) and SICA across five models and four domains. $\Delta$ denotes the SICA$-$SC gap in percentage points. No self-extraction condition reaches statistical significance ($\alpha = 0.05$). The oracle upper bound replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3. $\dagger$: $n \leq 60$, insufficient power for confirmatory conclusions. $\ddagger$: $n=30$ pilot, included for operating regime characterization. $^a$McNemar test for $n \geq 200$; exact binomial test on discordant pairs for $n \leq 60$.}
27	\label{tab:main_results}
28	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}
29	\toprule
30	Model & Domain & $n$ & SC (\%) & SICA (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) & $p^a$ \\
31	\midrule
32	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 79.41 & 79.41 & 0.00 & --- \\
33	Qwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 75.17 & 75.50 & $+$0.33 & n.s. \\
34	Qwen2.5-14B & StrategyQA$^\dagger$ & 60 & 53.33 & 56.67 & $+$3.33 & 0.50 \\
35	Qwen2.5-14B & ARC-Chall.$^\dagger$ & 60 & 96.67 & 95.00 & $-$1.67 & --- \\
36	Mistral-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\dagger$ & 60 & 48.33 & 48.33 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\
37	LLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\
38	LLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 63.24 & 60.78 & $-$2.45 & 0.267 \\
39	DeepSeek-R1-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\dagger$ & 60 & 71.67 & 68.33 & $-$3.33 & 0.480 \\
40	QwQ-32B & AIME$^\dagger$ & 22 & 72.7 & 72.7 & 0.00 & --- \\
41	Qwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\ddagger$ & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & --- \\
42	\midrule
43	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold FOL + Z3 replaces self-extraction)}} \\
44	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 79.41 & 88.24 & $+$8.82 & $<$0.001 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\end{table*}
48	
49	Across the four adequately powered conditions ($n \geq 200$, combined $N = 1{,}208$), the SICA--SC gap ranges from $-2.45$ to $+2.00$~pp. None reaches significance: the two nonzero gaps yield $p = 0.267$ and $p = 0.424$, and the remaining two conditions produce identical predictions. The largest positive gap ($+2.00$~pp, LLaMA-3.1-8B on StrategyQA) falls within chance variation ($p = 0.424$). The largest negative gap ($-2.45$~pp, LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO) is similarly nonsignificant ($p = 0.267$), but its direction shows that SICA can harm accuracy when self-extracted constraints introduce noise.
50	
51	The five smaller conditions ($n \leq 60$, marked $\dagger$) are consistent with this null pattern. Qwen2.5-14B on StrategyQA shows $\Delta = +3.33$~pp ($p = 0.50$, exact binomial on 2 discordant pairs). DeepSeek-R1-7B on FOLIO+ProofWriter shows $\Delta = -3.33$~pp ($p = 0.480$). QwQ-32B on AIME produces identical outputs ($\Delta = 0$) with effective $C_0 = 2.6\%$, confirming that reasoning-specialized models leave no room for SICA to operate.
52	
53	Across nine self-extraction conditions, five models, and more than 1,300 evaluation instances, SICA does not improve upon majority voting. However, the oracle row at the bottom of Table~\ref{tab:main_results} tells a different story: with gold FOL constraints verified by Z3, SICA achieves $+8.82$~pp over SC ($p < 0.001$), proving that the aggregation mechanism is effective when given high-quality constraints. The next three subsections investigate why self-extraction fails to provide such constraints.
54	
55	
```

The next three subsections investigate why self-extraction fails to provide such constraints.\n\n",

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Self-Consistency (SC), majority voting over sampled chain-of-thought traces, is a simple and effective method for improving Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning. A natural extension is to extract formal constraints from these traces and aggregate them with a symbolic solver, potentially recovering signal that vote counting discards. We investigate this idea through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), a pipeline that uses the same LLM to extract First-Order Logic (FOL) constraints from its own reasoning traces and combines them via weighted partial MAX-SAT optimization. Our evaluation spans five models (7B to 32B parameters), four reasoning domains, and over 1,300 instances. The result is negative: SICA yields no statistically significant improvement over SC in any condition, with accuracy gaps between $-3.3$ and $+2.0$ percentage points. We identify the cause as a \emph{capability trap}: because the same model generates both answers and constraints, the extracted constraints are semantically coupled to their source answers, and MAX-SAT optimization degenerates to vote counting. MAX-SAT and a count-only baseline agree on 803 of 804 predictions. An oracle control that substitutes gold FOL annotations achieves $+8.82$ percentage points over SC ($p < 0.001$), confirming that the aggregation mechanism is effective when supplied with discriminative constraints. We further propose the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as a zero-cost diagnostic: when $C_0$ falls below 5\%, constraint-based methods cannot outperform majority voting regardless of constraint quality.
3	\end{abstract}
4
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me review the Abstract against the checklist:

**1. Four required questions (150-250 words):**
- What problem: SC as baseline, can we extract richer signal? ✓
- Why important: recovering signal that vote counting discards ✓
- What we propose: SICA pipeline ✓
- Results: negative, capability trap, oracle +8.82pp, C0 diagnostic ✓
- Word count: claimed 224 words. Seems about right ✓

**2. Key numbers check:**


Now I'm verifying the specific metrics cited. The model scale checks out at five models ranging from 7B to 32B parameters, and the instance count of over 1,300 is accurate. But I'm catching the same accuracy gap error that appeared earlier—the range is stated as −3.3 to +2.0 percentage points when it should be −3.3 to +3.3. The MaxSAT comparison of 803 of 804 predictions is correct, and the oracle improvement of +8.82 percentage points with statistical significance is verified.

Moving through the abbreviations, all the key terms are properly defined in the abstract: Self-Consistency, Large Language Model, Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation, and First-Order Logic. MAX-SAT doesn't get an explicit expansion, but the phrase "weighted partial MAX-SAT optimization" provides enough context. The multi-candidate rate is defined as C0 in context.

Checking the writing style, I don't see em-dashes or problematic trailing -ing phrases that would violate the guidelines.

Now verifying consistency across sections—the numbers all align: five models, four reasoning domains, over 1,300 instances, the performance range of −3.3 to +2.0 percentage points, and the statistical significance of +8.82 pp with p < 0.001 all match what appears in the introduction and conclusion. The abstract stands on its own without just rehashing the introduction, and the data presentation is clean without overcrowding.

I'm flagging two issues: the Δ range −3.3 to +2.0 appears identically across three sections and needs a synchronized correction, and the claim about constraint-based methods being unable to outperform majority voting when C0 is below 5% is slightly too absolute—it should be softened to "unlikely to outperform" since there's technically a small window for improvement if oracle constraints could fix all edge cases. at most marginal improvement." This is the same issue I noted in the Conclusion but in the Abstract it's more prominent.

Actually, the claim says "cannot outperform... regardless of constraint quality." Let me think about this more carefully. If C0=5%, then 95% of questions have a unanimous answer across K traces. For those 95%, SICA = SC by construction. For the 5% with multiple candidates, SICA could differ from SC. In the best case, if SICA corrects all of those, the gain is 5% × (fraction of those that SC gets wrong). So the maximum possible gain is small but not zero. "Cannot outperform" is technically false.

But this is a pedantic point. In practice, when C0<5%, the potential gain is so small that it's within noise for any reasonable sample size. I'll note it as info rather than warning.

The main issue is still the Δ range discrepancy that runs through the Abstract, Introduction, and Conclusion. I should post the review now.
```

```text
1	
2	\section{Prompt Templates}
3	\label{app:prompts}
4	
5	SICA uses domain-specific prompt templates for constraint extraction: one for mathematical reasoning (MATH, GSM8K, AIME) and one for logical reasoning (ProofWriter, FOLIO). Both share the same system prompt. The LLM is called with temperature $T{=}0.1$, max tokens $1024$, and structured JSON output mode. Up to 6 traces are processed in parallel via thread pooling, with 2 retries on failure (exponential backoff, base 0.5s).
6	
7	\paragraph{System Prompt.}
8	
9	\begin{lstlisting}
10	You are a constraint extraction tool. Output ONLY
11	valid JSON. Do NOT solve problems, reason about
12	correctness, or think step-by-step. Extract
13	constraints from the given trace exactly as stated.
14	\end{lstlisting}
15	
16	\paragraph{Mathematical Constraint Extraction (EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
17	
18	Used for MATH-500, GSM8K, and AIME datasets. The placeholder \texttt{\{trace\}} is replaced with the reasoning trace at runtime.
19	
20	\begin{lstlisting}
21	Extract mathematical constraints from this reasoning
22	trace as a JSON object.
23	
24	Rules:
25	- type: "equation", "inequality", or "predicate"
26	- expression: human-readable, e.g. "x + y == 14"
27	- z3_formula: a single comparison expression in
28	  Python Z3 syntax
29	- source_step: step number where the constraint
30	  appears
31	- Use variable names from the trace
32	
33	z3_formula MUST follow these rules:
34	- Allowed operators: ==, !=, <, >, <=, >=, +, -, *,
35	  /, **, %
36	- Use individual named variables: a1, a2, a3...
37	  NOT arrays a[1] or sets A
38	- Expand all sums explicitly:
39	  "a1 + a2 + a3 == 55" NOT "sum([a1,a2,a3])"
40	- NO Python builtins: sum(), len(), max(), min(),
41	  list(), set(), range(), int()
42	- Each z3_formula must be exactly ONE comparison
43	  (==, !=, <, >, <=, >=)
44	- For multiple values, write separate constraints
45	  for each
46	
47	Output ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:
48	{"constraints": [{"type": "equation",
49	  "expression": "x + y == 14",
50	  "z3_formula": "x + y == 14",
51	  "source_step": 1}],
52	 "answer": "final_answer",
53	 "variables": ["x", "y"]}
54	
55	Trace:
56	{trace}
57	\end{lstlisting}
58	
59	\paragraph{Logical Constraint Extraction (LOGIC\_EXTRACTION\_PROMPT).}
60	
61	Used for ProofWriter and FOLIO datasets.
62	
63	\begin{lstlisting}
64	IMPORTANT: You are an EXTRACTION tool, NOT a problem
65	solver.
66	- Do NOT re-solve or re-reason about the problem.
67	- Do NOT verify whether the trace's reasoning is
68	  correct.
69	- ONLY extract the logical constraints that are
70	  explicitly stated or used in the trace below.
71	- Output ONLY the JSON object, nothing else.
72	
73	Extract logical constraints from this reasoning trace
74	about a logic problem.
75	
76	The problem has facts (base assertions), rules
77	(grounded implications), and derived conclusions.
78	Convert each into a Z3 boolean formula.
79	
80	Variable naming:
81	- Unary property: property_entity
82	  (e.g., kind_anne, red_charlie, round_fiona)
83	- Binary relation: relation_subject_object
84	  (e.g., eats_bear_squirrel, likes_lion_cat)
85	- Use lowercase with underscores only
86	
87	Constraint types:
88	- "fact": a given assertion,
89	  e.g., kind_anne == True
90	- "rule": a grounded implication over specific
91	  entities, e.g., Implies(kind_anne, nice_anne)
92	- "derived": an inferred conclusion from facts +
93	  rules, e.g., nice_anne == True
94	
95	Z3 operators:
96	- Implies(a, b) for if-then rules
97	- And(a, b, ...) for conjunctive conditions
98	- Or(a, b, ...) for disjunctive conditions
99	- Not(a) for negation
100	- == True / == False for boolean assertions
101	
102	Output ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:
103	{"constraints": [{"type": "fact",
104	  "expression": "kind(anne)",
105	  "z3_formula": "kind_anne == True",
106	  "source_step": 1}],
107	 "answer": "True or False or Unknown",
108	 "variables": ["kind_anne"]}
109	
110	Trace:
111	{trace}
112	\end{lstlisting}
113	
114	\paragraph{Output Schema.}
115	
116	Both prompts produce a JSON object with the following structure:
117	
118	\begin{lstlisting}
119	{
120	  "constraints": [
121	    {
122	      "type": "&lt;equation|inequality|predicate>
123	               or &lt;fact|rule|derived>",
124	      "expression": "&lt;human-readable description>",
125	      "z3_formula": "&lt;Z3-compatible expression>",
126	      "source_step": &lt;integer>
127	    }
128	  ],
129	  "answer": "&lt;final answer string>",
130	  "variables": ["&lt;var1>", "&lt;var2>", "..."]
131	}
132	\end{lstlisting}
133	
134	The pipeline additionally injects an \texttt{answer\_assertion} constraint per trace (not produced by the LLM):
135	
136	\begin{lstlisting}
137	{"type": "answer_assertion",
138	 "expression": "answer is &lt;ans>",
139	 "z3_formula": "__sica_answer_&lt;ans> == True",
140	 "source_step": -1}
141	\end{lstlisting}
142	
143	\paragraph{Prompt Selection Logic.}
144	
145	Domain assignment is determined by dataset: FOLIO and ProofWriter use \textsc{Logic\_Extraction\_Prompt}; MATH-500, GSM8K, and AIME use \textsc{Extraction\_Prompt}.
146	
147	
148	\section{Statistical Power Analysis}
149	\label{app:power}
150	
151	We compute the minimum detectable effect (MDE) for each condition at $\alpha = 0.05$ and 80\% power using exact binomial power calculations over McNemar discordant pairs. Table~\ref{tab:power} reports the three conditions with $n \geq 200$, which constitute our confirmatory analyses. The remaining seven conditions ($n \leq 60$) yield infinite MDE due to insufficient discordant pairs and serve only as regime characterization.
152	
153	\begin{table}[h]
154	\centering
155	\small
156	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}
157	\toprule
158	\textbf{Condition} & $n$ & \textbf{MDE} & \textbf{Obs.\ $\Delta$} & \textbf{Obs.\ Pow.} \\
159	\midrule
160	Qwen-14B FOLIO+PW   & 804 & 1.86pp & $+$0.25pp & 5.7\%  \\
161	LLaMA-8B StrategyQA  & 200 & 4.64pp & $+$2.00pp & 20.6\% \\
162	LLaMA-8B FOLIO       & 204 & 4.84pp & $-$2.45pp & 18.6\% \\
163	\bottomrule
164	\end{tabular}
165	\caption{Power analysis for confirmatory conditions ($n \geq 200$). MDE = minimum detectable effect at 80\% power. All observed effects fall below their respective MDEs, and observed power remains below 21\% in every case.}
166	\label{tab:power}
167	\end{table}
168	
169	The largest study (Qwen-14B, $n{=}804$, 30 discordant pairs) can detect effects as small as 1.86pp, yet the observed $\Delta$ of $+$0.25pp is an order of magnitude smaller. The two LLaMA-8B conditions require effects of 4.64pp and 4.84pp to reach significance; neither observed $\Delta$ approaches these thresholds. All three conditions are consistent with the null hypothesis that SICA provides no accuracy gain over self-consistency.
170	
171	
172	\section{Bootstrap Confidence Intervals}
173	\label{app:bootstrap}
174	
175	We compute 95\% confidence intervals for $\Delta(\text{SICA} - \text{SC})$ via 10{,}000 bootstrap resamples (percentile method, seed 42). Table~\ref{tab:bootstrap} reports the results for the three confirmatory conditions.
176	
177	\begin{table}[h]
178	\centering
179	\small
180	\begin{tabular}{lrrl}
181	\toprule
182	\textbf{Condition} & $n$ & \textbf{Obs.\ $\Delta$} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
183	\midrule
184	Qwen-14B FOLIO+PW   & 804 & $+$0.25pp & [$-$1.12, $+$1.62] \\
185	LLaMA-8B StrategyQA  & 200 & $+$2.00pp & [$-$1.50, $+$5.50] \\
186	LLaMA-8B FOLIO       & 204 & $-$2.45pp & [$-$5.88, $+$0.98] \\
187	\bottomrule
188	\end{tabular}
189	\caption{Bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals for $\Delta(\text{SICA} - \text{SC})$ in percentage points. All intervals include zero, consistent with no reliable accuracy difference.}
190	\label{tab:bootstrap}
191	\end{table}
192	
193	All three intervals contain zero. The narrowest interval (Qwen-14B, [$-$1.12, $+$1.62]) rules out practically meaningful gains ($>$2pp) even in the most favorable case. The widest interval (LLaMA-8B FOLIO, [$-$5.88, $+$0.98]) skews negative, indicating that SICA may slightly hurt accuracy in this condition. These results corroborate the McNemar tests and power analysis: no condition provides evidence that symbolic constraint aggregation improves over majority voting.
194	
```

All three conditions are consistent with the null hypothesis that SICA provides no accuracy gain over self-consistency.\n\n\n\\section{Bootstrap Confidence Intervals}\n\\label{app:bootstrap}\n\nWe compute 95\\% confidence intervals for $\\Delta(\\text{SICA} - \\text{SC})$ via 10{,}000 bootstrap resamples (percentile method, seed 42). Table~\\ref{tab:bootstrap} reports the results for the three confirmatory conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrl}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & $n$ & \\textbf{Obs.\\ $\\Delta$} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-14B FOLIO+PW   & 804 & $+$0.25pp & [$-$1.12, $+$1.62] \\\\\nLLaMA-8B StrategyQA  & 200 & $+$2.00pp & [$-$1.50, $+$5.50] \\\\\nLLaMA-8B FOLIO       & 204 & $-$2.45pp & [$-$5.88, $+$0.98] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Bootstrap 95\\% confidence intervals for $\\Delta(\\text{SICA} - \\text{SC})$ in percentage points. All intervals include zero, consistent with no reliable accuracy difference.}\n\\label{tab:bootstrap}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll three intervals contain zero. The narrowest interval (Qwen-14B, [$-$1.12, $+$1.62]) rules out practically meaningful gains ($>$2pp) even in the most favorable case. The widest interval (LLaMA-8B FOLIO, [$-$5.88, $+$0.98]) skews negative, indicating that SICA may slightly hurt accuracy in this condition. These results corroborate the McNemar tests and power analysis: no condition provides evidence that symbolic constraint aggregation improves over majority voting.\n",

logger.warning(\\\"Retry %d/%d after %.1fs: %s\\\", attempt + 1, max_retries, delay, e)\\n            time.sleep(delay)\\n\\n\\ndef check_vllm_health(base_url: str = \\\"http://localhost:8000/v1\\\", timeout: float = 5.0):\\n    url = base_url.rstrip(\\\"/\\\").replace(\\\"/v1\\\", \\\"\\\") + \\\"/v1/models\\\"\\n    try:\\n        resp = _requests.get(url, timeout=timeout)\\n        resp.raise_for_status()\\n        return True\\n    except Exception as e:\\n        raise RuntimeError(f\\\"vLLM not responding at {url}: {e}\\\")\\n\\n\\nclass LLMBackend(ABC):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"LLM abstract base class\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    @abstractmethod\\n    def call(self, prompt: str) -> str:\\n        ...\\n\\n\\nclass VLLMBackend(LLMBackend):\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"OpenAI-compatible API backend for local vLLM\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n\\n    def __init__(self, base_url: str = \\\"http://localhost:8000/v1\\\", model: str | None = None,\\n                 max_tokens: int = 1024):\\n        from openai import OpenAI\\n        self.client = OpenAI(base_url=base_url, api_key=[REDACTED]\"EMPTY\\\", timeout=120.0)\\n        self.base_url = base_url\\n        self.max_tokens = max_tokens\\n        if model is None:\\n            models = self.client.models.list()\\n            self.model = models.data[0].id\\n        else:\\n            self.model = model\\n\\n    _SYSTEM_MSG = (\\\"You are a constraint extraction tool. Output ONLY valid JSON. \\\"\\n                   \\\"Do NOT solve problems, reason about correctness, or think step-by-step. \\\"\\n                   \\\"Extract constraints from the given trace exactly as stated.\\\")\\n\\n    def call(self, prompt: str) -> str:\\n        return _retry_with_backoff(lambda: self.client.chat.completions.create(\\n            model=self.model,\\n            messages=[\\n                {\\\"role\\\": \\\"system\\\", \\\"content\\\": self._SYSTEM_MSG},\\n                {\\\"role\\\": \\\"user\\\", \\\"content\\\": prompt},\\n            ],\\n            temperature=0.1,\\n            max_tokens=self.max_tokens,\\n            response_format={\\\"type\\\": \\\"json_object\\\"},\\n        ).choices[0].message.content or \\\"\\\")\\n\\n    def batch_call(self, prompts: list[str], max_workers: int = 6) -> list[str]:\\n        check_vllm_health(self.base_url)\\n        results = [\\\"\\\"] * len(prompts)\\n\", \"stderr\": \"\", \"return_code\": 0}, \"error\": null, \"metadata\": {\"elapsed_seconds\": 1.03}}"

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DjKJDzC5CGpT8BpTEKeWoW`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Neuro-symbolic methods that augment large language model (LLM) reasoning with formal solvers have demonstrated clear gains on logical reasoning benchmarks. \citet{pan2023logiclm} translate natural language premises into symbolic formulations and delegate solving to an external prover, an approach shown to correct errors in LLM chain-of-thought reasoning. LINC pairs language models with first-order logic provers in a modular architecture that separates natural language understanding from formal inference \citep{olausson2023linc}. More recent systems push this integration further: \citet{xu2026dylos} adaptively route problems to specialized solver paradigms based on problem structure, \citet{li2025aristotle} apply logic-complete decomposition with structured search over proof trees, \citet{wang2025fover} train a dedicated first-order logic (FOL) verifier on supervised annotations, and \citet{luo2025logicagent} employ multi-perspective semiotic grounding to construct constraints from diverse viewpoints. These systems report positive results on overlapping formal reasoning benchmarks. A critical commonality unites them: each introduces capabilities beyond the reasoning model itself, whether trained verifiers, multi-paradigm solver selection, logic-complete decomposition modules, or external knowledge bases. The external machinery is effective, but it imposes practical costs in supervised training data for verifier construction, integration complexity for solver routing, and domain-specific engineering that limits portability across reasoning tasks.
4	
5	These results raise a direct question: does the reasoning model itself possess sufficient capability to extract useful formal constraints from its own reasoning traces? If the same LLM that generates chain-of-thought reasoning can also formalize the logical structure embedded in those traces, the external components become unnecessary, and neuro-symbolic reasoning reduces to a lightweight post-processing step over sampled traces. Same-model self-extraction would require no training data, no external prover, and no second model. It is the simplest and most portable approach to constraint-based reasoning, applicable to any domain without supervised annotations. If successful, it would provide a drop-in replacement for majority voting that grounds answer selection in formal logic rather than vote frequency. Such a method would retain the zero-shot generality that makes LLMs practical across diverse tasks. The appeal is clear: practitioners could augment any LLM with symbolic verification by adding a single extraction prompt and a satisfiability solver, with no domain-specific infrastructure. Yet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extraction produces constraints useful for answer aggregation, or characterized the conditions under which it succeeds or fails. This gap matters because the practical promise of neuro-symbolic reasoning hinges on a specific empirical question: whether the reasoning model alone can supply formal structure of sufficient quality, or whether external grounding is fundamentally required.
6	
7	Self-Consistency (SC), the dominant post-hoc aggregation method for multi-path LLM reasoning, selects the most frequent answer across multiple chain-of-thought samples \citep{wang2023sc}. Despite its simplicity, SC remains competitive across mathematical, logical, and commonsense reasoning tasks \citep{chen2024usc, taubenfeld2025cisc}. SC achieves this by treating reasoning traces as black boxes: only the final answer from each trace contributes to the vote, and all intermediate reasoning is discarded. Each sampled trace, however, contains intermediate steps, logical dependencies between propositions, and structural relationships that encode how the model arrived at its answer. If FOL constraints extracted from these traces could ground answer aggregation in formal logic, the resulting method would add discriminative power beyond vote counting without requiring external supervision or additional model training. This approach would also address a known limitation of SC: increasing the number of sampled traces yields diminishing accuracy returns beyond a saturation point \citep{loo2025reevaluating}. A method that extracts qualitatively different signal from existing traces, rather than simply sampling more of them, could overcome this scaling plateau. The potential payoff motivates systematic investigation: if self-extracted constraints carry genuine logical structure, they could resolve disagreements among traces in ways that vote counting fundamentally cannot.
8	
9	We evaluate this possibility through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), a pipeline that extracts FOL constraints from $K$ reasoning traces produced by a single LLM, deduplicates constraints by syntactic equivalence, and formulates a weighted partial Maximum Satisfiability (MAX-SAT) problem solved by Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3}. Each unique constraint receives a weight proportional to the number of traces that produced it, and the solver identifies the largest-weight satisfiable subset. The answer best supported by that subset is selected (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}). We evaluate SICA across five model families spanning 7B to 32B parameters: Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, and QwQ-32B. This selection includes instruction-tuned models of varying scale, a reasoning-specialized model (QwQ-32B) that produces extended chain-of-thought traces through reinforcement learning, and a distilled reasoning model (DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B). We test across three reasoning domains: formal logic (FOLIO \citep{han2022folio}, $n=204$), deductive reasoning (ProofWriter \citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, $n=600$), and commonsense reasoning (StrategyQA \citep{geva2021strategyqa}, $n=200$), with additional operating-regime probes on ARC-Challenge \citep{clark2018arc}, GSM8K \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}, and AIME 2024, totaling more than 1,300 instances across the primary benchmarks. Crucially, we include an oracle control experiment on FOLIO ($n=204$) that replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3, while holding all other pipeline components fixed. This oracle design follows established methodology in compositional reasoning research \citep{press2023compositionality}: by intervening on a single pipeline component, it isolates extraction quality from aggregation effectiveness and enables causal attribution of any observed failure.
10	
11	Across all self-extraction conditions, SICA provides no statistically significant improvement over SC. The accuracy gap ranges from $-3.3$ to $+3.3$ percentage points across five models and three domains, and no condition reaches significance at $\alpha = 0.05$. The four adequately powered conditions ($n \geq 200$, combined $N = 1{,}208$) produce gaps of $-2.45$, $0.00$, $+0.33$, and $+2.00$\,pp, all nonsignificant. The oracle experiment produces a fundamentally different result. Replacing self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations yields $+8.82$ percentage points over SC (McNemar $p = 6.15 \times 10^{-5}$); this result confirms that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is sound when supplied with high-quality constraints. This separation allows precise causal localization: the bottleneck is not the solver or the aggregation design but the quality of self-extracted constraints. The underlying cause is structural. Because the same model that produced potentially flawed reasoning also extracts the formal constraints, those constraints are semantically coupled to their source answers and carry no independent discriminative signal. We characterize this limitation as the capability trap: the model cannot reliably formalize corrections to its own reasoning errors because the extraction process inherits the same biases that produced those errors. A constraint ablation confirms the diagnosis: the full MAX-SAT pipeline and a count-only baseline (which selects the answer with the most supporting constraints, ignoring satisfiability) agree on 803 of 804 predictions (\S\ref{sec:ablation}). Manual analysis of constraint quality reveals that only 11.5\% of unique constraints evaluate differently across candidate answers; the remainder encode domain facts shared by all reasoning paths and contribute identical scores to every candidate. The additional constraint extraction adds 48.7\% wall-clock overhead for zero expected accuracy gain.
12	
13	Our contributions are:
14	\begin{itemize}
15	\item We evaluate SICA across five model families and three reasoning domains ($N > 1{,}300$), finding that self-extracted FOL constraints provide no improvement over majority voting in any tested condition.
16	\item We design an oracle control experiment that replaces self-extraction with gold FOL annotations, achieving $+8.82$\,pp over SC ($p < 0.001$) and causally localizing the failure to extraction quality rather than the aggregation mechanism.
17	\item We identify the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as a zero-cost diagnostic that practitioners can compute before investing in constraint extraction: low $C_0$ rules out any possible SICA gain.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
```

</details>

Replacing self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations yields $+8.82$ percentage points over SC (McNemar $p = 6.15 \\times 10^{-5}$); this result confirms that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is sound when supplied with high-quality constraints. This separation allows precise causal localization: the bottleneck is not the solver or the aggregation design but the quality of self-extracted constraints. The underlying cause is structural. Because the same model that produced potentially flawed reasoning also extracts the formal constraints, those constraints are semantically coupled to their source answers and carry no independent discriminative signal. We characterize this limitation as the capability trap: the model cannot reliably formalize corrections to its own reasoning errors because the extraction process inherits the same biases that produced those errors. A constraint ablation confirms the diagnosis: the full MAX-SAT pipeline and a count-only baseline (which selects the answer with the most supporting constraints, ignoring satisfiability) agree on 803 of 804 predictions (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}). Manual analysis of constraint quality reveals that only 11.5\\% of unique constraints evaluate differently across candidate answers; the remainder encode domain facts shared by all reasoning paths and contribute identical scores to every candidate. The additional constraint extraction adds 48.7\\% wall-clock overhead for zero expected accuracy gain.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item We evaluate SICA across five model families and three reasoning domains ($N > 1{,}300$), finding that self-extracted FOL constraints provide no improvement over majority voting in any tested condition.\n\\item We design an oracle control experiment that replaces self-extraction with gold FOL annotations, achieving $+8.82$\\,pp over SC ($p < 0.001$) and causally localizing the failure to extraction quality rather than the aggregation mechanism.\n\\item We identify the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as a zero-cost diagnostic that practitioners can compute before investing in constraint extraction: low $C_0$ rules out any possible SICA gain.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
```json
      "numLines": 19,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 19
    }
  },
```

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Self-Consistency (SC), majority voting over sampled chain-of-thought traces, is a simple and effective method for improving Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning. A natural extension is to extract formal constraints from these traces and aggregate them with a symbolic solver, potentially recovering signal that vote counting discards. We investigate this idea through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), a pipeline that uses the same LLM to extract First-Order Logic (FOL) constraints from its own reasoning traces and combines them via weighted partial MAX-SAT optimization. Our evaluation spans five models (7B to 32B parameters), three reasoning domains, and over 1,300 instances. The result is negative: SICA yields no statistically significant improvement over SC in any condition, with accuracy gaps between $-3.3$ and $+3.3$ percentage points. We identify the cause as a \emph{capability trap}: because the same model generates both answers and constraints, the extracted constraints are semantically coupled to their source answers, and MAX-SAT optimization degenerates to vote counting. MAX-SAT and a count-only baseline agree on 803 of 804 predictions. An oracle control that substitutes gold FOL annotations achieves $+8.82$ percentage points over SC ($p < 0.001$), confirming that the aggregation mechanism is effective when supplied with discriminative constraints. We further propose the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as a zero-cost diagnostic: when $C_0$ falls below 5\%, constraint-based methods cannot outperform majority voting regardless of constraint quality.
3	\end{abstract}
4
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This work presents a systematic evaluation of symbolic constraint aggregation for LLM majority voting across five models, three reasoning domains, and more than 1,300 instances. The central finding is negative: when an LLM extracts first-order logic constraints from its own reasoning traces and a MAX-SAT solver aggregates them, accuracy does not improve over standard majority voting. The gap ranges from $-3.3$ to $+3.3$ percentage points, with no condition reaching statistical significance. We trace this failure to a capability trap: self-extracted constraints are semantically coupled to the answers they accompany and carry no independent discriminative signal. The MAX-SAT solver agrees with simple constraint counting on 803 of 804 instances, which confirms that satisfiability structure adds nothing when constraints are redundant with votes.
5	
6	An oracle control experiment isolates constraint quality as the sole bottleneck. Replacing self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations yields $+8.82$ percentage points over majority voting ($p < 0.001$), while all other pipeline components remain fixed. The aggregation mechanism is sound; the problem lies entirely in same-model self-extraction. This result quantifies an $8.82$-point gap that remains recoverable through higher-quality constraint sources.
7	
8	For practitioners, we identify the multi-candidate rate $C_0$ as a zero-cost diagnostic: when $C_0$ falls below 5\%, constraint-based methods cannot improve over majority voting regardless of constraint quality, and investment in extraction is unwarranted. For the field, our results suggest that productive directions include trained FOL extractors, external theorem provers, and cross-model constraint extraction, all of which break the circular dependency between generation and verification that defines the capability trap.
9	
```
