<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_012TBPTAEMyuvv3sKUZiTcPK`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	The oracle experiment (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, bottom) is not merely an upper bound on SICA performance; it constitutes a causal intervention that isolates the source of failure. By replacing only the constraint source (from self-extracted to gold FOL annotations) while holding all other pipeline components fixed, we establish that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is effective when supplied with discriminative constraints. The +8.82pp improvement ($p < 0.001$) confirms that aggregation is not the bottleneck. This oracle design follows established methodology in compositional reasoning research: \citet{press2023compositionality} use oracle sub-question decompositions to isolate compositionality gaps, \citet{trivedi2023ircot} employ oracle retrieval to separate retrieval from reasoning errors, and \citet{saparov2023greedy} supply gold FOL proofs to test reasoning capacity independent of formalization skill. Our oracle applies the same logic to constraint extraction, pinpointing same-model self-extraction as the single point of failure.
5	
6	A closer examination of constraint quality explains the mechanism behind this failure. In a manual analysis of 43 problems from the pilot dataset, 23 of which had multiple candidate answers, only 11.5\% of unique constraints (37.2\% by frequency-weighted mass) evaluated differently across candidates. The remaining constraints encoded domain facts shared by all reasoning paths: predicate definitions (\texttt{kind(charlie) = True}), background implications (\texttt{Implies(round(x), kind(x))}), and intermediate inference steps that hold regardless of the final answer. Because these constraints are satisfied by every candidate equally, they contribute identical scores to the MAX-SAT objective and cancel during aggregation. The effective flip rate confirms this diagnosis: in only 4 of 23 multi-answer problems (17.4\%) did discriminative constraints favor a non-majority answer. This pattern accounts for the near-perfect agreement between MAX-SAT and count-only aggregation (803/804, Table~\ref{tab:ablation}). The solver receives overwhelmingly non-discriminative input and defaults to the majority signal.
7	
8	The computational cost of this non-discriminative extraction is substantial. SICA adds 48.7\% wall-clock overhead relative to SC, with constraint extraction consuming 47.0\% of total pipeline time while MAX-SAT solving accounts for less than 0.01\%. The overhead is dominated by an additional LLM call per trace for FOL extraction, adding approximately 30 seconds per problem across the 804-problem evaluation. SICA and SC agree on 773 of 804 predictions; the 31 disagreements yield a net gain of only 2 problems (16 SICA wins, 14 SC wins, 1 mutual error). The expected accuracy improvement per unit of additional compute is effectively zero.
9	
10	Several recent systems report positive results on overlapping benchmarks using symbolic constraints. DyLoS \citep{xu2026dylos} achieves gains through multi-paradigm solver selection that routes problems to appropriate formal systems. Aristotle \citep{li2025aristotle} applies logic-complete decomposition that breaks complex premises into independently verifiable units. FoVer \citep{wang2025fover} trains a dedicated FOL verifier on supervised data, so the reasoning model need not extract its own constraints. LogicAgent \citep{luo2025logicagent} employs multi-perspective grounding to construct constraints from diverse viewpoints. The common thread is that none of these methods rely on same-model self-extraction. Their success is complementary to our negative finding, not contradictory. The oracle result confirms that the gap these methods bridge (up to +8.82pp) is real and recoverable through external means.
11	
12	The +8.82pp oracle ceiling quantifies the potential that remains locked behind the capability trap. Three concrete paths can unlock this potential. First, trained FOL extractors, supervised on datasets with gold annotations such as FOLIO, can learn extraction patterns that exceed same-model capabilities. Second, external provers that verify constraints independently of the reasoning model eliminate the circular dependency between generation and verification. Third, cross-model extraction, where a separate model extracts constraints from another model's reasoning traces, breaks the self-reference loop that produces non-discriminative constraints. Each approach targets the extraction bottleneck identified by our ablation: since MAX-SAT and count-only produce identical predictions on 803 of 804 instances (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), improving the aggregation mechanism offers no path forward.
13	
14	Our analysis also reveals $C_0$ (the multi-candidate rate) as a zero-cost diagnostic for practitioners considering constraint-based voting. $C_0$ is computable directly from the trace sampling stage, before any constraint extraction occurs. When $C_0$ falls below 5\%, as with QwQ-32B ($C_0 = 2.6\%$), SICA degenerates to SC by construction because nearly all questions receive unanimous answers. High $C_0$ is necessary but not sufficient for SICA to outperform SC, since the capability trap can render extracted constraints non-discriminative even when candidate disagreement is abundant (e.g., LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, $C_0 = 61.8\%$, $\Delta = 0$). Practitioners should compute $C_0$ from the sampling stage before investing in constraint extraction: low $C_0$ rules out any possible gain, while high $C_0$ warrants further investigation of constraint quality before deployment.
15	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe oracle experiment (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}, bottom) is not merely an upper bound on SICA performance; it constitutes a causal intervention that isolates the source of failure. By replacing only the constraint source (from self-extracted to gold FOL annotations) while holding all other pipeline components fixed, we establish that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is effective when supplied with discriminative constraints. The +8.82pp improvement ($p < 0.001$) confirms that aggregation is not the bottleneck. This oracle design follows established methodology in compositional reasoning research: \\citet{press2023compositionality} use oracle sub-question decompositions to isolate compositionality gaps, \\citet{trivedi2023ircot} employ oracle retrieval to separate retrieval from reasoning errors, and \\citet{saparov2023greedy} supply gold FOL proofs to test reasoning capacity independent of formalization skill. Our oracle applies the same logic to constraint extraction, pinpointing same-model self-extraction as the single point of failure.\n\nA closer examination of constraint quality explains the mechanism behind this failure. In a manual analysis of 43 problems from the pilot dataset, 23 of which had multiple candidate answers, only 11.5\\% of unique constraints (37.2\\% by frequency-weighted mass) evaluated differently across candidates. The remaining constraints encoded domain facts shared by all reasoning paths: predicate definitions (\\texttt{kind(charlie) = True}), background implications (\\texttt{Implies(round(x), kind(x))}), and intermediate inference steps that hold regardless of the final answer. Because these constraints are satisfied by every candidate equally, they contribute identical scores to the MAX-SAT objective and cancel during aggregation. The effective flip rate confirms this diagnosis: in only 4 of 23 multi-answer problems (17.4\\%) did discriminative constraints favor a non-majority answer. This pattern accounts for the near-perfect agreement between MAX-SAT and count-only aggregation (803/804, Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}). The solver receives overwhelmingly non-discriminative input and defaults to the majority signal.\n\nThe computational cost of this non-discriminative extraction is substantial. SICA adds 48.7\\% wall-clock overhead relative to SC, with constraint extraction consuming 47.0\\% of total pipeline time while MAX-SAT solving accounts for less than 0.01\\%. The overhead is dominated by an additional LLM call per trace for FOL extraction, adding approximately 30 seconds per problem across the 804-problem evaluation. SICA and SC agree on 773 of 804 predictions; the 31 disagreements yield a net gain of only 2 problems (16 SICA wins, 14 SC wins, 1 mutual error). The expected accuracy improvement per unit of additional compute is effectively zero.\n\nSeveral recent systems report positive results on overlapping benchmarks using symbolic constraints. DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos} achieves gains through multi-paradigm solver selection that routes problems to appropriate formal systems. Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle} applies logic-complete decomposition that breaks complex premises into independently verifiable units. FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover} trains a dedicated FOL verifier on supervised data, so the reasoning model need not extract its own constraints. LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent} employs multi-perspective grounding to construct constraints from diverse viewpoints. The common thread is that none of these methods rely on same-model self-extraction. Their success is complementary to our negative finding, not contradictory. The oracle result confirms that the gap these methods bridge (up to +8.82pp) is real and recoverable through external means.\n\nThe +8.82pp oracle ceiling quantifies the potential that remains locked behind the capability trap. Three concrete paths can unlock this potential. First, trained FOL extractors, supervised on datasets with gold annotations such as FOLIO, can learn extraction patterns that exceed same-model capabilities. Second, external provers that verify constraints independently of the reasoning model eliminate the circular dependency between generation and verification. Third, cross-model extraction, where a separate model extracts constraints from another model's reasoning traces, breaks the self-reference loop that produces non-discriminative constraints. Each approach targets the extraction bottleneck identified by our ablation: since MAX-SAT and count-only produce identical predictions on 803 of 804 instances (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), improving the aggregation mechanism offers no path forward.\n\nOur analysis also reveals $C_0$ (the multi-candidate rate) as a zero-cost diagnostic for practitioners considering constraint-based voting. $C_0$ is computable directly from the trace sampling stage, before any constraint extraction occurs. When $C_0$ falls below 5\\%, as with QwQ-32B ($C_0 = 2.6\\%$), SICA degenerates to SC by construction because nearly all questions receive unanimous answers. High $C_0$ is necessary but not sufficient for SICA to outperform SC, since the capability trap can render extracted constraints non-discriminative even when candidate disagreement is abundant (e.g., LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, $C_0 = 61.8\\%$, $\\Delta = 0$). Practitioners should compute $C_0$ from the sampling stage before investing in constraint extraction: low $C_0$ rules out any possible gain, while high $C_0$ warrants further investigation of constraint quality before deployment.\n",

```text
Found the sources:

1. **48.7% overhead**: From `paper_table_data.json`, the `sica_overhead_pct` = 48.7. This is the combined SICA overhead across ProofWriter and FOLIO. The breakdown shows:
   - Trace generation: 48.7% (ProofWriter) / 61.4% (FOLIO) / 51.3% (Combined)
   - Constraint extraction: 49.6% (ProofWriter) / 37.2% (FOLIO) / 47.0% (Combined)
   - SICA overhead (extraction + dedup + MAX-SAT): 51.3% (ProofWriter) / 38.6% (FOLIO) / 48.7% (Combined)

The Discussion confirms this—SICA adds 48.7% wall-clock overhead relative to SC, with constraint extraction consuming 47.0% of the total pipeline time. The overhead calculation appears to be the time spent on extraction, deduplication, and MAX-SAT solving relative to the trace generation baseline. So the 48.7% overhead is actually measured as a fraction of total SICA time, not relative to the SC baseline—meaning the extra work (extraction, dedup, MAX-SAT) accounts for about 48.7% of the entire pipeline, while trace generation takes up the remaining 51.3%. This interpretation makes sense: SICA is roughly twice as slow as SC because it's adding nearly as much time as the baseline trace generation itself requires. overhead relative to SC" is ambiguous. It could mean:
   (a) SICA takes 48.7% more time than SC → SICA time = 1.487 × SC time
   (b) 48.7% of SICA's total time is overhead beyond SC → SICA time = SC time / 0.513 ≈ 1.95 × SC time
   
   Interpretation (b) would mean SICA is ~95% slower than SC, not 48.7% slower. The Introduction says "adds 48.7% wall-clock overhead" which strongly implies interpretation (a). But the data supports interpretation (b): SICA total time / SC time = 100/51.3 ≈ 1.95x.

Now I'm looking at the actual overhead percentages from the JSON data. The ProofWriter SICA overhead is 51.3%, FOLIO is 38.6%, and combined is 48.7%. When I calculate ProofWriter's trace generation at 32.0s and extraction at 33.6s for a total of 65.6s, the overhead relative to just trace generation would be 105%. But the JSON's 51.3% figure actually represents the fraction of total time spent on SICA-specific processing—that's 33.6/65.6—not a percentage increase relative to SC. So the paper's phrasing about "adds 48.7% wall-clock overhead relative to SC" is misleading given what the numbers actually show. The phrasing "48.7% overhead relative to SC" is ambiguous—it could mean either 48.7% of total time or a 48.7% increase, but the actual ratio suggests SICA takes roughly 1.95x the time of SC, making the wording potentially misleading. For the 11.5% discriminative constraints figure, I can't find it in the analysis files, only in an unrelated CSV row, so it likely came from a separate manual analysis of 43 pilot problems that the author computed during writing rather than from a saved output file.

The real issue is that the Introduction cites this 11.5% statistic but it only appears in the Discussion section, not in the Experiments where it should be established—the Introduction either needs to reference the Discussion explicitly or the Experiments section should include this analysis to properly ground the claim.

I'm also noticing the Δ = 0 notation for LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO in Discussion P6 still hasn't been corrected, and I need to verify whether the "802 of 804" claim about MAX-SAT and count-only predictions is accurate.

Looking at the numbers more carefully, the new Discussion properly distinguishes between the MaxSAT versus count-only comparison (803 of 804 instances) and the SICA versus SC comparison (773 of 804 predictions), which is a significant improvement. However, I'm flagging two persistent issues: the LLaMA FOLIO Δ value should be -2.45 rather than 0, and the 11.5% discriminative constraints finding from the Experiments section still isn't being cited in the Discussion where it's relevant. in Introduction P5 and Discussion P2, but not established in the Experiments section. Either the Experiments should include this analysis or the Introduction should reference the Discussion section.

The 48.7% overhead phrasing could be misinterpreted as a percentage increase over SC when it actually represents the overhead component of SICA's total runtime, which is roughly 1.95x SC's time.

Domain count and Δ range are now consistent across all sections at three reasoning domains and -3.3 to +3.3. The agreement metrics have been corrected to 773/804 for SICA vs SC and 803/804 for MaxSAT vs Count-only in the appropriate sections. The section label change from sec:intro to sec:introduction resolved cleanly without any broken references.

{
  "text": "## 审查报告 — Introduction v2 + Discussion v2（修订版联合审查）\n\n**结论**: WARNING（1 个数据溯源问题 + 1 个旧 warning 未修）\n\n### 已修复确认\n\n- ✅ **Δ 范围** 全文同步为 \"−3.3 to +3.3 pp\"（Abstract、Introduction、Conclusion、Experiments 均一致）\n- ✅ **Domain count** 全文同步为 \"three reasoning domains\"，GSM8K/ARC/AIME 定位为 \"operating-regime probes\"\n- ✅ **Discussion P3 正确报告 SICA vs SC = 773/804**（31 disagreements, 16 SICA wins, 14 SC wins, 1 mutual error），与 exp013_agreement_matrix.json 完全一致\n- ✅ **Discussion P5 正确报告 MaxSAT vs Count-only = 803/804**\n- ✅ **sec:intro → sec:introduction** label 变更无断引用\n\n### 新发现\n\n- [severity: warning] **\"11.5% discriminative constraints\" 无可追溯数据文件**。Introduction P5 和 Discussion P2 均引用此数字。Discussion P2 描述来源为 \"manual analysis of 43 problems from the pilot dataset\"。服务器 `analysis/` 目录无对应数据文件（搜索 `*constraint*quality*`、`*pilot*`、`*manual*` 均为空，`paper_table_data.json` 中也无此数字）。审稿人若要求 underlying data 无法提供。建议：(a) 补跑分析脚本生成 JSON 文件并注册到 registry，或 (b) 至少在 Appendix 记录分析方法和原始数据。同样适用于 37.2%（frequency-weighted）和 17.4%（4/23 flip rate）。\n\n- [severity: warning] **Discussion P6 \"LLaMA FOLIO Δ=0\" 仍未修复**。第一次 Discussion 审查已报告此问题。Discussion line 14: \"LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, $C_0 = 61.8\\%$, $\\Delta = 0$\"。Table 2 报告 Δ=-2.45pp。建议改为 \"$\\Delta = -2.45$~pp (n.s.)\"。\n\n- [severity: info] **\"48.7% wall-clock overhead\" 数据来源已确认**：`paper_table_data.json` 含 `sica_overhead_pct: 48.7`。但注意：此数字是 SICA 额外工作占 SICA 总时间的比例（48.7%/100%），而非相对 SC 的增量百分比。SC 时间占 SICA 总时间 51.3%，实际 SICA ≈ 1.95× SC。\"adds 48.7% overhead relative to SC\" 的表述容易被理解为 \"比 SC 慢 48.7%\"（实际慢 ~95%）。建议改为 \"SICA roughly doubles SC wall-clock time\" 或 \"constraint extraction accounts for 48.7% of total pipeline time\"。\n\n- [severity: info] **Introduction 叙事结构改进显著**。Oracle-led narrative（正面发现引领 → 失败定位到 self-extraction）比前版更具说服力。适合 negative-result paper 的 framing。\n\n### 全文 CRITICAL/WARNING 更新追踪\n\n| # | 位置 | 问题 | Status |\n|---|------|------|--------|\n| 1 | experiments.tex Table 3 | 802/804 + Random-wt acc | ⏳ 待修 |\n| 2 | related_work.tex L11 | DeepSeek-R1 C0<5% | ⏳ 待修 |\n| 3 | discussion.tex P6 | LLaMA FOLIO Δ=0 | ⏳ 待修 |\n| 4 | Introduction + Discussion | 11.5% 无数据文件 | ⏳ 需补 |\n| 5 | abstract + intro + conclusion | Δ 范围 + domain count | ✅ 已修 |",

```text
T=0.7 ablation is done! But wait, the result says only partial completion — earlier I saw 38/60 complete, and the file was 189KB (vs 565-642KB for others). But the worker reports "DONE" with metrics. Let me verify:

- SC accuracy: 75.00% (45/60)
- SICA accuracy: 71.67% (43/60)
- Δ: -3.33pp
- C0: 40.0%
- Discordant: 0 SICA wins, 2 SC wins (total 2 discordant)


The worker was dispatched to diagnose the experiment, which means it analyzed whatever data was available at the time. The "DONE (negative)" status and the cleaned-up cron suggest the monitoring stopped, but I need to confirm whether these metrics are based on all 60 problems or just the partial 38 that were complete when I last checked.

Now I'm looking at the C0 progression across temperature values: 30% at T=0.3, 38.3% at T=0.5, 40.0% at T=0.7, and 43.3% at T=1.0. The previous T=0.7 result of 53.3% from a different run was creating non-monotonicity, but this new consistent-source measurement of 40.0% restores the monotonic trend—which matters for the paper's narrative. However, SICA actually underperforms SC at T=0.7 by 3.33 percentage points with no SICA wins versus two SC wins, confirming the capability trap where SICA can degrade performance rather than improve it. I should verify this against the raw data.

_source line 1150 · 2026-05-17T19:14:03.009Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PHsP8RzGFWWjNnr6KD6f8f`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Neuro-symbolic methods propose extracting first-order logic (FOL) constraints from Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning traces to improve answer aggregation beyond majority voting. Whether the reasoning model itself can produce useful constraints through self-extraction has not been systematically evaluated. If self-extraction works, it eliminates the need for trained verifiers, external provers, or domain-specific infrastructure. We evaluate Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which formulates a weighted partial Maximum Satisfiability (MAX-SAT) problem over self-extracted FOL constraints, across five model families (7B to 32B parameters) and three reasoning domains with more than 1,300 total instances. We include an oracle control that replaces self-extraction with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3 to isolate extraction quality from aggregation effectiveness. Self-extracted SICA provides no statistically significant improvement over Self-Consistency (SC) in any tested condition. The oracle variant achieves $+8.82$ percentage points over SC ($p < 0.001$), which proves the aggregation mechanism is sound when supplied with high-quality constraints. We identify this failure as a \emph{capability trap}: because the same model that produced potentially flawed reasoning also extracts the constraints, those constraints are semantically coupled to their source answers and carry no independent discriminative signal. Only 11.5\% of self-extracted constraints evaluate differently across candidate answers. This result establishes that same-model constraint extraction cannot improve LLM answer aggregation without independent sources of formal knowledge.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a revised Abstract (v2). Let me compare with the previous version and review.

**Changes from v1:**
1. Opens with "neuro-symbolic methods" and the research question instead of SC background
2. Oracle-positive framing (mentions oracle control earlier)
3. "three reasoning domains" (consistent with other sections now) ✓
4. "more than 1,300 total instances" ✓
5. Drops the Δ range entirely (no more "-3.3 to +3.3")
6. Drops "803 of 804" agreement number


7. Adds "11.5% of self-extracted constraints evaluate differently" 
8. Drops C0 diagnostic from Abstract
9. ~216 words (within 150-250 range) ✓

Now I'm verifying the key claims: the +8.82 percentage point improvement over SC checks out, the model range from 7B to 32B parameters is accurate, the three reasoning domains align with the rest of the paper, the 1,300+ instance count is correct, and the 11.5% statistic on constraint evaluation variance is confirmed.

Removing C0 from the Abstract makes sense as a prioritization call—it's still listed in the contributions section, and the Abstract benefits from the tighter focus. Same reasoning applies to dropping the "803 of 804" detail and the delta range; these cuts reduce clutter without losing substance.

I should verify the four required elements are present: the research problem about systematic evaluation of self-extraction is clearly stated, the importance of eliminating external dependencies comes through, the SICA approach with oracle control is the proposed method, and the results section addresses the negative findings for self-extraction.

The style checks look solid—no em-dashes, no -ing constructions, all abbreviations defined. But I'm flagging that "11.5%" still needs a traceable data source, same issue as before. More importantly, the closing statement about self-extraction being unable to improve aggregation without independent knowledge sources is too absolute given the experimental scope. The paper only tested models up to 32B, and the Limitations section itself notes this scale constraint, so the claim should be softened to reflect that larger models or different strategies might eventually succeed.

Actually, wait—rereading this more carefully, that statement *is* the paper's central thesis. The oracle results prove independent constraints work while all self-extraction approaches fail. In an Abstract, some boldness in framing the core finding is justified, even if it's technically bounded by the paper's scope. Reviewers might push back on the absoluteness, but the claim is defensible given what the experiments actually show.

This revision is clean. The only remaining issue is that 11.5% data traceability problem I've already flagged.
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:exp-setup}
6	
7	We evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across five models and three reasoning domains, with two additional benchmarks as operating-regime probes.
8	
9	\paragraph{Models.} We test Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct~\cite{yang2024qwen25}, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct~\cite{dubey2024llama3}, DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B~\cite{guo2025deepseekr1}, and QwQ-32B~\cite{qwen2024qwq}, spanning 7B to 32B parameters.
10	
11	\paragraph{Datasets.} FOLIO~\cite{han2022folio} provides formal logic problems with gold FOL annotations ($n=204$), enabling the oracle control (\S\ref{sec:oracle}). ProofWriter~\cite{tafjord2021proofwriter} tests multi-step deductive reasoning ($n=600$). StrategyQA~\cite{geva2021strategyqa} evaluates commonsense reasoning ($n=200$ for LLaMA-3.1-8B; $n=60$ for other models). ARC-Challenge~\cite{clark2018arc} covers science questions ($n=60$). GSM8K~\cite{cobbe2021gsm8k} ($n=30$) and AIME 2024 ($n=22$) serve as operating-regime probes, both exhibiting near-ceiling SC accuracy or near-zero $C_0$.
12	
13	\paragraph{Parameters.} We sample $K=12$ traces per question at temperature $T=0.7$. Z3~\cite{demoura2008z3} solves each MAX-SAT instance with a 10-second timeout. Temperature ablation varies $T \in \{0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$.
14	
15	\paragraph{Metrics.} We report accuracy (\%), $C_0$ (\S\ref{sec:c0}), and $\Delta = \text{SICA} - \text{SC}$ in percentage points (pp). Statistical significance uses the McNemar test at $\alpha = 0.05$. For conditions with $n \geq 200$, we compute 95\% bootstrap confidence intervals with 10{,}000 resamples.
16	
17	
18	\subsection{Main Results: SICA Provides No Significant Gain}
19	\label{sec:main-results}
20	
21	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports results across all model-domain conditions. Symbolic constraint aggregation does not improve over majority voting.
22	
23	\begin{table*}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\caption{Accuracy of Self-Consistency (SC) and SICA across three primary domains and two supplementary probes. $\Delta$ denotes the SICA$-$SC gap in percentage points. No self-extraction condition reaches statistical significance ($\alpha = 0.05$). The oracle upper bound replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3. $\dagger$: $n \leq 60$, insufficient power for confirmatory conclusions. $\ddagger$: $n=30$ pilot, included for operating regime characterization. $^a$McNemar test for $n \geq 200$; exact binomial test on discordant pairs for $n \leq 60$.}
27	\label{tab:main_results}
28	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}
29	\toprule
30	Model & Domain & $n$ & SC (\%) & SICA (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) & $p^a$ \\
31	\midrule
32	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 79.41 & 79.41 & 0.00 & --- \\
33	Qwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 75.17 & 75.50 & $+$0.33 & n.s. \\
34	Qwen2.5-14B & StrategyQA$^\dagger$ & 60 & 53.33 & 56.67 & $+$3.33 & 0.50 \\
35	Qwen2.5-14B & ARC-Chall.$^\dagger$ & 60 & 96.67 & 95.00 & $-$1.67 & --- \\
36	Mistral-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\dagger$ & 60 & 48.33 & 48.33 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\
37	LLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\
38	LLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 63.24 & 60.78 & $-$2.45 & 0.267 \\
39	DeepSeek-R1-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\dagger$ & 60 & 71.67 & 68.33 & $-$3.33 & 0.480 \\
40	QwQ-32B & AIME$^\dagger$ & 22 & 72.7 & 72.7 & 0.00 & --- \\
41	Qwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\ddagger$ & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & --- \\
42	\midrule
43	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold FOL + Z3 replaces self-extraction)}} \\
44	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 79.41 & 88.24 & $+$8.82 & $<$0.001 \\
45	\bottomrule
46	\end{tabular}
47	\end{table*}
48	
49	Across the four adequately powered conditions ($n \geq 200$, combined $N = 1{,}208$), $\Delta$ ranges from $-2.45$ to $+2.00$~pp, with no condition reaching significance ($p = 0.267$ and $0.424$ for the two nonzero gaps). The six smaller conditions ($n \leq 60$) are consistent with this null pattern: $\Delta$ ranges from $-3.33$ to $+3.33$~pp, all nonsignificant. QwQ-32B on AIME produces identical outputs ($C_0 = 2.6\%$), confirming that reasoning-specialized models leave no room for SICA to operate.
50	
51	Across ten conditions, five models, and more than 1,300 instances, SICA does not improve upon majority voting. The oracle row tells a different story: gold FOL constraints yield $+8.82$~pp over SC ($p < 0.001$), proving the aggregation mechanism works when given discriminative constraints.
52	
53	
54	\subsection{Oracle Control: The Aggregation Mechanism Is Effective}
55	\label{sec:oracle}
56	
57	We construct an oracle variant that replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations from FOLIO ($n = 204$). Z3 evaluates ground-truth formulas against each candidate answer; all other pipeline components remain identical.
58	
59	Oracle-SICA achieves 88.24\% accuracy versus 79.41\% for both SC and standard SICA. The $+8.82$~pp gain is highly significant (McNemar $p = 6.15 \times 10^{-5}$). Z3 checks on gold annotations agree with ground-truth labels for 193/204 problems (94.6\%). This establishes that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given correct constraints, and that the bottleneck lies entirely in extraction quality.
60	
61	
62	\subsection{Capability Trap Diagnostics}
63	\label{sec:diagnostics}
64	
65	Why does SICA fail? Full per-condition metrics are in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:capability_trap}. $C_0$ ranges from 2.6\% (QwQ-32B) to 61.8\% (LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO). For reasoning-specialized models, over 97\% of questions receive unanimous answers, so SICA reduces to SC by construction. For instruction-tuned models, higher $C_0$ creates opportunities for SICA to differ from SC, yet $\Delta$ remains nonsignificant everywhere (Figure~\ref{fig:diagnostics}b). Extraction rates exceed 0.80 and contradiction rates stay below 7\%, so the pipeline is mechanically functional. The problem is extraction vacuity: constraints are syntactically valid but semantically redundant with their source answers. Even the highest-quality condition (LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, extraction rate 0.94) yields $\Delta = -2.45$~pp. A temperature ablation ($T \in \{0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$, Qwen2.5-14B, $n{=}60$) shows $C_0$ increasing monotonically from 30.0\% ($T{=}0.3$) to 38.3\% ($T{=}0.5$), 40.0\% ($T{=}0.7$), and 43.3\% ($T{=}1.0$), yet $\Delta$ never exceeds zero: 0.0~pp at $T{=}0.3$, 0.0~pp at $T{=}0.5$, $-3.33$~pp at $T{=}0.7$ ($n{=}60$, n.s.), and 0.0~pp at $T{=}1.0$ (Figure~\ref{fig:diagnostics}a). Even at the default temperature $T{=}0.7$, SICA does not outperform SC.
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\columnwidth}
70	\centering
71	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig2_capability_scatter.pdf}
72	\caption{Vote entropy vs.\ constraint exclusion rate (Qwen2.5-14B, $n{=}307$ multi-candidate problems).}
73	\label{fig:capability_scatter}
74	\end{subfigure}
75	\hfill
76	\begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\columnwidth}
77	\centering
78	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig3_operating_regime.pdf}
79	\caption{$C_0$ vs.\ SICA$-$SC gap across all conditions.}
80	\label{fig:operating_regime}
81	\end{subfigure}
82	\caption{Capability trap diagnostics. (a) High vote entropy does not benefit from constraint filtering. (b) The accuracy gap stays centered at zero regardless of answer diversity.}
83	\label{fig:diagnostics}
84	\end{figure}
85	
86	
87	\subsection{Constraint Ablation: MAX-SAT Degenerates to Counting}
88	\label{sec:ablation}
89	
90	If self-extracted constraints are non-discriminative, MAX-SAT should produce the same predictions as baselines ignoring satisfiability. We test three variants on Qwen2.5-14B (FOLIO + ProofWriter, $n = 804$): full SICA, count-only (most constraints wins, ignoring satisfiability), and random-weight (randomized weights before solving).
91	
92	\begin{table}[t]
93	\centering
94	\small
95	\caption{Constraint ablation (Qwen2.5-14B, $n = 804$, FOLIO + ProofWriter). Agreement: predictions identical to SC out of 804.}
96	\label{tab:ablation}
97	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrc@{}}
98	\toprule
99	Aggregation & Acc.\ (\%) & Agreement w/ SC \\
100	\midrule
101	SC (majority vote) & 76.24 & --- \\
102	SICA (MAX-SAT) & 76.49 & 773\,/\,804 \\
103	Count-only & 76.62 & 774\,/\,804 \\
104	Random-weight & 75.87 & 764\,/\,804 \\
105	\bottomrule
106	\end{tabular}
107	\end{table}
108	
109	MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 803 of 804 predictions (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming the degeneration predicted in Equation~\ref{eq:degenerate}. Against SC, all three variants change 30--40 predictions, but wins and losses cancel (MAX-SAT: 16 wins, 14 losses among 31 discordant pairs). Self-extracted constraints carry no discriminative signal beyond vote counts. The oracle control ($+8.82$~pp) confirms that the aggregation mechanism works given independent problem structure; self-extracted constraints simply fail to provide it.
110

The oracle upper bound replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations verified by Z3. $\\dagger$: $n \\leq 60$, insufficient power for confirmatory conclusions. $\\ddagger$: $n=30$ pilot, included for operating regime characterization. $^a$McNemar test for $n \\geq 200$; exact binomial test on discordant pairs for $n \\leq 60$.}\n\\label{tab:main_results}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Domain & $n$ & SC (\\%) & SICA (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p^a$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 79.41 & 79.41 & 0.00 & --- \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 75.17 & 75.50 & $+$0.33 & n.s. \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & StrategyQA$^\\dagger$ & 60 & 53.33 & 56.67 & $+$3.33 & 0.50 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & ARC-Chall.$^\\dagger$ & 60 & 96.67 & 95.00 & $-$1.67 & --- \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\\dagger$ & 60 & 48.33 & 48.33 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 63.24 & 60.78 & $-$2.45 & 0.267 \\\\\nDeepSeek-R1-7B & FOLIO+PW$^\\dagger$ & 60 & 71.67 & 68.33 & $-$3.33 & 0.480 \\\\\nQwQ-32B & AIME$^\\dagger$ & 22 & 72.7 & 72.7 & 0.00 & --- \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\\ddagger$ & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & --- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold FOL + Z3 replaces self-extraction)}} \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 79.41 & 88.24 & $+$8.82 & $<$0.001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nAcross the four adequately powered conditions ($n \\geq 200$, combined $N = 1{,}208$), $\\Delta$ ranges from $-2.45$ to $+2.00$~pp, with no condition reaching significance ($p = 0.267$ and $0.424$ for the two nonzero gaps). The six smaller conditions ($n \\leq 60$) are consistent with this null pattern: $\\Delta$ ranges from $-3.33$ to $+3.33$~pp, all nonsignificant. QwQ-32B on AIME produces identical outputs ($C_0 = 2.6\\%$), confirming that reasoning-specialized models leave no room for SICA to operate.\n\nAcross ten conditions, five models, and more than 1,300 instances, SICA does not improve upon majority voting. The oracle row tells a different story: gold FOL constraints yield $+8.82$~pp over SC ($p < 0.001$), proving the aggregation mechanism works when given discriminative constraints.\n\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control: The Aggregation Mechanism Is Effective}\n\\label{sec:oracle}\n\nWe construct an oracle variant that replaces self-extracted constraints with gold FOL annotations from FOLIO ($n = 204$). Z3 evaluates ground-truth formulas against each candidate answer; all other pipeline components remain identical.\n\nOracle-SICA achieves 88.24\\% accuracy versus 79.41\\% for both SC and standard SICA. The $+8.82$~pp gain is highly significant (McNemar $p = 6.15 \\times 10^{-5}$). Z3 checks on gold annotations agree with ground-truth labels for 193/204 problems (94.6\\%). This establishes that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given correct constraints, and that the bottleneck lies entirely in extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Capability Trap Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nWhy does SICA fail? Full per-condition metrics are in Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:capability_trap}. $C_0$ ranges from 2.6\\% (QwQ-32B) to 61.8\\% (LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO). For reasoning-specialized models, over 97\\% of questions receive unanimous answers, so SICA reduces to SC by construction. For instruction-tuned models, higher $C_0$ creates opportunities for SICA to differ from SC, yet $\\Delta$ remains nonsignificant everywhere (Figure~\\ref{fig:diagnostics}b). Extraction rates exceed 0.80 and contradiction rates stay below 7\\%, so the pipeline is mechanically functional. The problem is extraction vacuity: constraints are syntactically valid but semantically redundant with their source answers. Even the highest-quality condition (LLaMA-3.1-8B on FOLIO, extraction rate 0.94) yields $\\Delta = -2.45$~pp. A temperature ablation ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$, Qwen2.5-14B, $n{=}60$) shows $C_0$ increasing monotonically from 30.0\\% ($T{=}0.3$) to 38.3\\% ($T{=}0.5$), 40.0\\% ($T{=}0.7$), and 43.3\\% ($T{=}1.0$), yet $\\Delta$ never exceeds zero: 0.0~pp at $T{=}0.3$, 0.0~pp at $T{=}0.5$, $-3.33$~pp at $T{=}0.7$ ($n{=}60$, n.s.), and 0.0~pp at $T{=}1.0$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:diagnostics}a). Even at the default temperature $T{=}0.7$, SICA does not outperform SC.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{fig2_capability_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Vote entropy vs.\\ constraint exclusion rate (Qwen2.5-14B, $n{=}307$ multi-candidate problems).}\n\\label{fig:capability_scatter}\n\\end{subfigure}\n\\hfill\n\\begin{subfigure}[b]{0.48\\columnwidth}\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{fig3_operating_regime.pdf}\n\\caption{$C_0$ vs.\\ SICA$-$SC gap across all conditions.}\n\\label{fig:operating_regime}\n\\end{subfigure}\n\\caption{Capability trap diagnostics. (a) High vote entropy does not benefit from constraint filtering. (b) The accuracy gap stays centered at zero regardless of answer diversity.}\n\\label{fig:diagnostics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\n\\subsection{Constraint Ablation: MAX-SAT Degenerates to Counting}\n\\label{sec:ablation}\n\nIf self-extracted constraints are non-discriminative, MAX-SAT should produce the same predictions as baselines ignoring satisfiability. We test three variants on Qwen2.5-14B (FOLIO + ProofWriter, $n = 804$): full SICA, count-only (most constraints wins, ignoring satisfiability), and random-weight (randomized weights before solving).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Constraint ablation (Qwen2.5-14B, $n = 804$, FOLIO + ProofWriter). Agreement: predictions identical to SC out of 804.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrc@{}}\n\\toprule\nAggregation & Acc.\\ (\\%) & Agreement w/ SC \\\\\n\\midrule\nSC (majority vote) & 76.24 & --- \\\\\nSICA (MAX-SAT) & 76.49 & 773\\,/\\,804 \\\\\nCount-only & 76.62 & 774\\,/\\,804 \\\\\nRandom-weight & 75.87 & 764\\,/\\,804 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nMAX-SAT and count-only agree on 803 of 804 predictions (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming the degeneration predicted in Equation~\\ref{eq:degenerate}. Against SC, all three variants change 30--40 predictions, but wins and losses cancel (MAX-SAT: 16 wins, 14 losses among 31 discordant pairs). Self-extracted constraints carry no discriminative signal beyond vote counts. The oracle control ($+8.82$~pp) confirms that the aggregation mechanism works given independent problem structure; self-extracted constraints simply fail to provide it.\n",

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related-work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Self-Consistency and Extensions.}
5	Self-Consistency (SC) selects the most frequent answer from multiple sampled chain-of-thought traces \citep{wang2023sc} and remains a strong baseline for multi-path reasoning. Universal Self-Consistency (USC) extends SC to free-form generation by having the LLM itself select the most consistent response \citep{chen2024usc}. Confidence-weighted variants assign trace-level confidence scores to improve vote quality \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, while DiVeRSe combines diverse prompting strategies with step-aware verification \citep{li2023diverse}. Recent work has identified scaling plateaus: increasing the number of sampled traces yields diminishing returns beyond a saturation point \citep{loo2025reevaluating}. These findings motivate the search for aggregation mechanisms that extract richer signal from reasoning traces than simple vote counting. SICA represents one such attempt: it replaces plurality voting with symbolic constraint satisfaction over self-extracted FOL formulas. Our negative results show that the bottleneck lies not in the aggregation mechanism but in the quality of self-extracted constraints, a failure mode that vote-based methods sidestep by not requiring formalization at all.
6	
7	\paragraph{Neuro-Symbolic Reasoning.}
8	A growing line of work combines LLMs with symbolic solvers to improve logical reasoning. Logic-LM translates natural language problems into symbolic formulations and delegates solving to an external prover \citep{pan2023logiclm}. LINC pairs language models with first-order logic provers in a modular architecture \citep{olausson2023linc}. More recent systems push this integration further: DyLoS adaptively selects among multiple solver paradigms \citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle decomposes problems through a logic-complete search framework \citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer trains a dedicated verifier on FOL annotations \citep{wang2025fover}, and LogicAgent applies multi-perspective semiotic grounding \citep{luo2025logicagent}. A critical distinction separates these methods from SICA. All of the above rely on trained verifiers, external provers, or multi-paradigm solver selection, components that introduce knowledge beyond the reasoning model itself. SICA instead asks whether the same model that produced the reasoning traces can also extract useful formal constraints from them. Our oracle experiment confirms that sound FOL constraints do improve aggregation (+8.82\,pp); the failure is specific to same-model self-extraction, not to symbolic reasoning as an approach.
9	
10	\paragraph{Test-Time Compute and Reasoning Models.}
11	Scaling test-time computation has emerged as an alternative to parameter scaling. \citet{snell2025scaling} show that optimally allocated test-time compute can outperform larger models and establish a framework for analyzing the compute-accuracy tradeoff at inference. Reasoning-specialized models such as DeepSeek-R1 \citep{deepseek2025r1} internalize extended chain-of-thought through reinforcement learning and produce longer but more coherent traces. Diversity-aware training methods explicitly optimize for trace diversity during test-time scaling \citep{diverseaware2025}. These developments interact with our findings through the $C_0$ diagnostic. Reasoning models produce highly consistent outputs across samples: QwQ-32B achieves $C_0 = 2.6\%$ on our benchmarks, while DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($C_0 = 58.3\%$) behaves as an instruction-scale model rather than a reasoning model, likely because its 7B capacity cannot sustain the deterministic reasoning chains learned through distillation. When nearly all traces converge to the same answer, any aggregation method reduces to the unanimous output. The $C_0$ metric thus provides a zero-cost signal for practitioners to determine whether post-hoc aggregation can offer any benefit for a given model.
12	
13	\paragraph{Verification and Reranking.}
14	Trained verifiers offer an alternative path to improving multi-path reasoning. Outcome-level reward models (ORMs) score complete solutions and select the highest-ranked candidate \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}. Process reward models (PRMs) assign step-level scores and enable finer-grained selection \citep{lightman2023prm}. Math-Shepherd automates process supervision annotation and removes the need for human labels \citep{wang2024mathshepherd}. Reasoning-aware variants score individual reasoning paths rather than training separate verifiers: RASC assigns confidence scores based on textual quality indicators and consistency \citep{wan2024rasc}, while ReASC frames adaptive sampling as evidence sufficiency and aggregates response-level confidence \citep{kim2026reasc}. These approaches share SICA's goal of selecting better answers from a candidate pool but differ in that they either train a separate scoring model or use lightweight heuristics grounded in textual features rather than formal logic. SICA's constraint extraction requires no training data and no additional model; it relies entirely on the reasoning model's own ability to formalize its logic. Our results suggest that this self-reliance is precisely the problem. Without an external signal to ground constraint quality, self-extracted constraints encode the same biases present in the original traces and fail to provide corrective information.
15	
16	\paragraph{Constraint Satisfaction in NLP.}
17	Constraint-based formulations have gained traction for evaluating and improving LLM reasoning. SATBench benchmarks LLMs on Boolean satisfiability problems and reveals systematic weaknesses in logical structure handling \citep{satbench2025}. Work on integrating LLMs with constraint propagation solvers explores hybrid architectures where the LLM generates candidate assignments and a CP solver enforces hard constraints \citep{llmmeets_cp2025}. SICA's MAX-SAT formulation sits within this broader trend: it uses Z3 to find the maximum-weight satisfiable subset of self-extracted constraints. The key difference is that SICA's constraints originate from the model's own reasoning traces rather than from problem specifications or external knowledge bases. Our finding that these self-extracted constraints lack discriminative power complements the SATBench results: LLMs struggle both to solve formal constraints directly and to produce useful constraints from their own reasoning.
18	
19	\paragraph{Debate and Multi-Agent Aggregation.}
20	Multi-agent debate refines answers through iterative natural language discussion among LLM instances \citep{liang2023encouraging,du2023improving}. In debate, models exchange arguments in plain text and update their positions based on peer responses. SICA takes a structurally different approach: instead of natural language refinement, it extracts formal constraints and resolves disagreements through satisfiability optimization. Both families attempt to move beyond simple voting, but debate preserves natural language flexibility while SICA commits to formal structure. \citet{huang2024selfcorrect} show that LLMs struggle to self-correct reasoning without external feedback, a finding that parallels our capability trap: the same model that produced flawed reasoning cannot reliably formalize the correction. Across all six lines of work reviewed here, the consistent pattern is that improving on majority voting requires information beyond what the reasoning model can extract from its own outputs, whether from trained verifiers, external solvers, human annotations, or cross-model interaction.
```
